Measured both ways: 13 offered in survival against 24 in sandbox, and with an empty survival pack the identical seven-part build sequence places NOTHING and refuses seven times by name. | Accepted | 2026-07-26 |
| GP-36 | **THE FOUNDING PLANE IS CHOSEN TO FIT ITS FOOTPRINT (DW-33).** `StructureTolerance.fitPlane(lo, hi, floatM, buryM)` returns `clamp(hi - buryM, lo, lo + floatM)`, and `makeSite` moves the site plane by it. Three regimes: a spread inside BURY keeps the old rule exactly (`d = lo`, everything buried, which is how a base looks best); a spread between BURY and BURY+FLOAT spends the bury budget to the last millimetre and hangs only the remainder; a spread past the budget leaves the plane where the float side is exactly satisfied, so the refusal names the HIGH ground, which is the half Q can cut. **Measured, same 81 origins out to 6.4 km, one probe run: 14.8% of origins buildable under the pinned rule against 58.0% under the fit** (3.9x), and the `mid` site went 70.6% to 100.0% of its 812 footprints. **`PLANE_MARGIN` = 2%, and the first version without it was BROKEN.** Spending the bury budget to the last millimetre puts the worst point at exactly `buryM`, and `checkGround` re-derives that number by a different arithmetic path: both are `ground(x) - |x|`, a difference of two doubles of magnitude 600,000, so the cancellation carries about 1e-10 m and a slack ratio of 1 + 2e-10 is greater than 1. On the coarse height field this lane started against, spreads mostly fitted inside the bury bound so the exact-fit branch was rarely taken and the defect hid; the hour the terrain lane gave the noise stack real detail (WG-25) it became the normal case and **every one of 88 scanned candidate sites refused with the sentence about the ground standing 0.50 m into it, against a 0.50 m bound**. 2% rather than an epsilon because there are two problems and only one is arithmetic: a plane exactly on the bury bound also leaves the SECOND cell of the site no room at all. It costs 10 mm of an invisible budget. On the terrain that was shipped when the work started the spawn itself read **0 of 812 footprints buildable** with the bury side reading 1.0123 m p50 against a 0.50 m bound. **Bias towards BURYING rather than splitting the error proportionally**, which DW-33 permits and which is not a wash: both accept exactly the same ground, because feasibility is `spread <= FLOAT + BURY` for every choice inside the band, but they differ in which side the SECOND and later cells of the site land on, and the two sides are not equally rescuable. A float past the bound can be carried by a neighbour (GP-38); a bury past it has no relief but a shovel. So the precious budget is spent first and the renewable one is spilled into. | The old constant did not encode a tolerance, it encoded "the error all lands on one side", which is a SCALE ASSUMPTION in disguise: defensible at a 1 m module where a footprint spread 0.127 m, and silently a bug at 4 m where the same ground spreads 1.01. `probes/buildtol.js` now scores the pinned rule and the fit side by side in one run over the same footprints, so the before and the after are one measurement rather than two runs a day apart, and a regression to the old rule cannot pass quietly. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-37 | **A NEW PART PREFERS AN EXISTING PART'S SOCKET TO THE BARE GRID, and the ghost NAMES the socket it caught.** `game/StructureSnap.ts`: the four `socket_edge_n/e/s/w` on a deck and the two `socket_end_l/r` on a wall are read out of the shipped `.glb` files ONCE at load; the nearest one within `cellM * 0.75` (3.00 m) overrules the grid address whenever the cell it offers is free. A deck's edge offers a WALL that edge and another DECK the cell across it; a wall's end offers the next wall along the same line, inheriting the run's flip. **Nothing here maps a socket by its NAME**: `socket_edge_e` is at local +X in the asset's frame and a deck yawed onto a site has local +X pointing site WEST, so the direction comes from the socket's own local position rotated by the part's quaternion and resolved against the site's east and north. **Measured (`probes/basesnap.js`, driven, at least one placement through a real `PointerEvent`): deck to deck gap 1.392e-12 m against the module read off the socket; a wall's origin 0.000e+0 m from the deck socket the ghost SAID it caught, and 0.000e+0 m from the nearest one, which are two separate assertions; two panels of a run 0.000e+0 m end to end.** | The arithmetic was never wrong and that is exactly why the complaint survived: adjacent foundations already met at 1.2e-12 m (GP-22), so every existing probe was green while Reid reported that nothing snapped. What was missing is that the address came from wherever the AIM RAY stopped, and a ray pointed at a placed foundation stops on its top face, so the answer was "already built here" for the whole 4 x 4 m of the thing you were standing on. The structures lane had published the machinery for this in ASSET-SPECS 4.23 and never wired it. Reading the socket rather than recomputing `cellM * 0.5` is what makes the claim measurable at all: an anchor recomputed from the module agrees with itself whatever the assets ship. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-38 | **THE CANTILEVER EXISTS. A deck with an orthogonally adjacent deck on the same level and site may hang one STOREY (4.00 m, read off `module.storey`) instead of `FLOAT_TOLERANCE_M` (0.90 m), the run is capped at 3 cells from the nearest deck that genuinely rests on the ground, and BURY IS NOT RELAXED.** `carryRun` is a breadth-first walk over the deck lattice recomputed per ghost frame rather than a stored depth: no save-schema change, and a base whose hillside has since been mined away is re-judged honestly, which is GP-28's live-ground argument applied to support. `restsOnGround` re-measures a placed deck's own footprint against the live oracle. **Measured (`probes/cantilever.js`, on ground the probe scanned for rather than assumed, 1.07 m of drop per cell): cells at runs 1, 1, 2 and 3 accepted hanging 0.50, 2.00, 3.35 and 3.91 m, the fourth refused at run -1 with "nothing under this and no solid ground within 3 cells", and DW-32's pillar drawn under the overhang at 3 decks / 9 pieces / 3.152 m tallest.** THE NEGATIVE CONTROL IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING: the same cell, `d:0,2,0`, with its three carrying decks pulled up and nothing else changed, hangs the identical 2.0014 m and is refused with "it would hang 2.00 m clear". | `supported()` returned `true` unconditionally for every level-0 deck, so DW-32's headline feature had no neighbour concept behind it at all. The cap is not pedantry: with a pillar drawn under it a cantilever reads as engineering, and without a bound a player steps one deck at a time across a canyon for ever, which is the thing the pillar was meant to make honest rather than to enable. The negative control is what makes the other two assertions mean anything, because a build that had simply raised `floatToleranceM` to one storey for everything would pass both of them perfectly; doing it on the SAME cell rather than on some other one is what removes the ground from the comparison, leaving only whether a neighbour was standing there. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-39 | **A MACHINE PLACED OVER A DECK SITS ON THE DECK'S TOP SURFACE, and the hand furnace finally leaves /core's voxel lattice.** `MachinePlacement.anchorIn` asks the base `deckTopAt(site, e, n, u)` and takes the level's base plane plus the asset's own `socket_top` height when a deck covers the cell, with the SITE's up rather than the local radial so the machine is flush with the plane instead of with the sphere it is tangent to. **The two lattices are NOT re-unified**: the structural cell stays 4 m and `MACHINE_TILE_M` stays 1 m, deliberately different constants sharing one site frame, and the machine's tangent coordinates are simply divided down into the structural cell containing them. `MachineAddr.u` carries the aim's own height so a furnace aimed at the ground floor of a two-storey base lands on the ground floor. **Measured: `onDeck` true and 6.7e-12 m above the deck's own `socket_top`.** | "Items like smelters dont sit ontop of the foundation" was exactly true and it was one line: a foundation deliberately writes nothing to the voxel layer (DW-24), so a machine taking its radius from `of_surface_radius` stood on the ground UNDER the deck and sank half a metre into it. The second half is that `Machines.ts` was the LAST thing in the build system still snapping to `of_cell_for_pos` after GP-27 moved drills, belts and factory smelters onto the site frame, so a hand furnace and the belt running into it disagreed about where a metre starts by up to 0.41 m. The lattice fallback is not kept for a host-less client: a grid nothing else uses any more is worse than no grid, so that path is the live oracle at the point itself. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-40 | **STRUCTURAL COSTS RE-PRICED AT THE 4 m MODULE: foundation 40 Stone, floor 20 Wood + 20 Stone, wall 12 Wood, door 16 Wood + 4 Iron.** 10x on the two decks and 4x on the two wall parts, **deliberately short of area parity**. `structureDefs()` in `gameplay.h`; `Demolition` refunds follow for free because they read the same def. **Measured end to end in survival: 40 Stone charged per foundation, read from /core's own quoted cost rather than from a literal in the probe, and a short pack refused by name with "need 16 Wood + 4 Iron".** | DW-32 made every part sixteen times the area in plan, so a 20 x 20 m platform silently fell from 400 foundations at 1,600 Stone to 25 at 100: a 16x discount nobody chose. Full parity would have restored 1,600 and made the rescale purely cosmetic, and DW-32's stated purpose was to cut the grind rather than to relabel it, so 10x lands the same platform at 1,000. The two families scale differently because they are different shapes: a deck is an AREA and grew 16x, a wall is a LINE and grew 5.6x (1 m to 4 m wide, 2.50 m to 3.50 m tall), so 4x is the same discount applied to the same growth. The door's iron goes 1 to 4 because iron is the gate on a door and a gate that did not move would have been a 16x discount on the only interesting ingredient in the set. The `/core` test now pins the RELATION (a deck costs more than a wall, a door costs more than a wall) as well as the values, because the values will be rebalanced again and the ordering is the design decision. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-41 | **Player progression is a NEW header, `core/include/of/progression.h`, not a section of `gameplay.h`.** Additive and header-only over `gameplay.h`, exactly the pattern `research.h` established, carrying equipment, skills and appearance plus a `PlayerProfile` that holds all three so persistence has one thing to serialise and one thing to version. `progression_tests`: 10 tests / 823 checks. | Two reasons, and the second is the durable one. **(1)** `gameplay.h` had three lanes editing it in one night (survival content, the smelting ladder, the structural set) and a fourth simultaneous editor is how commits start sweeping each other, which happened to this very lane's re-price. **(2)** The layering is genuinely one-way: equipment reads the item registry and nothing in the item registry needs to know equipment exists, so a separate header states that rather than relying on section discipline inside a 1,400-line file. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-42 | **FOUR EQUIP SLOTS (head, chest, legs, feet), armour ItemIds at 0x0070+, and the mesh node name is a PURE FUNCTION OF THE SLOT.** `armourNode(EquipSlot)` returns the same four strings `assets/models/dist/player/armour_set.glb` ships, because the art lane's own contract says the node names are SLOT names and not SET names, so a second armour set is a second file carrying the same four nodes and nothing in the client moves. A suit SUMS damage reduction, MULTIPLIES encumbrance and is capped at `kMaxDamageReduction` 0.80; the tier-1 iron set totals 0.40 reduction at **0.8940393** move speed (GP-52: this row said 0.892 for a night, which was a transcription of the table below it rather than the table). **The ItemId block deliberately SKIPS 0x0044..0x006F**: 0x0050..0x006A is already spoken for by GP-31's vessel part items (`ItemId = 0x0050 + (PartId - 0x0100)`), which live in `web/wasm/of_vessel_api.inc` only because that lane could not touch `/core` and are due to be promoted. Minting armour on top of them would turn a scheduled promotion into a renumbering of saved worlds, and an ItemId is opaque (GP-13) so a gap costs exactly nothing. **Armour publishes `damageAfter(raw)` and does not apply it**, because `/core` has no damage model yet and the enemies lane is building one; a layer that silently changed numbers under twenty pinned tests would be a worse gift than an interface. | The art lane shipped four skinned armour meshes and left the honest note in `contracts.json`: "NO REFERENT IN THE HEADLESS HEADERS ... grepping core/include for armor\|armour\|equip returns nothing." This is that referent. The equip guard against a displaced piece that will not fit back CANNOT FIRE while armour stacks to one, because removing the incoming piece empties exactly the slot the outgoing one needs, and the test says so out loud rather than leaving a branch nobody can explain: `unequip` is the direction that genuinely refuses, because it adds without removing. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-43 | **Five skills, one per verb the game ALREADY has (Mining, Forestry, Smelting, Building, Piloting), a QUADRATIC curve (`100 * n^2`, cap 10), and LEVEL 0 IS EXACTLY NEUTRAL. Appearance is five bytes of PALETTE INDICES, not free colour.** `skillMultiplier(0) == 1.0f` bit-exactly, which is what makes the whole layer optional: a caller that never grants a point gets the numbers it has always had, so `harvestNode` and `Furnace` can opt in through `Skills::applyYield` without any pinned test moving. Rounding is DOWN and is published as one call rather than left as `int(n * mul)` at four call sites, three of which would round the other way within a month. | A skill with no action behind it is a line in a menu, so there are five and not fifteen. Quadratic rather than exponential because an exponential last level costs more than the whole game before it, and this project's progression spine is the tech tree rather than a level bar. A fixed palette is five bytes in a save, is trivially validated by `sanitise` (which is idempotent, so a load-save-load cycle cannot walk a player's face across the palette), gives the art lane a finite set of combinations to actually look at, and cannot produce a player the exact grey of the rock they are standing on, which is free RGB's most likely first use. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-44 | **THE TECH TREE IS THE ONLY AUTHORITY ON WHAT IS GATED: an item, entity or recipe is locked IF AND ONLY IF some tech in the tree names it, and everything the tree never mentions is free for ever.** `TechTree::gatesItem/gatesEntity/gatesRecipe` plus `ResearchState::isItemAvailable/isEntityAvailable/isRecipeAvailable`; "unlocked" and "available" are deliberately different questions and every gate in the client asks the second. | The obvious design is a locked-by-default list beside an unlock list, and it is two authorities that fall out of step in the first week: somebody adds a machine, forgets the list, and it ships either permanently locked or permanently free with no error either way. Deriving "gated" FROM the tree makes adding a tech the whole edit, and it is why nobody ever had to write down that wood is not locked. It is ModalStack's derivation argument and GP-29's named-question argument applied to progression, which is the third time this shape has paid for itself. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-45 | **THE SURVIVAL TECH TREE IS A SECOND TREE (research.h section D, TechIds 0x0010+), NOT AN EDIT OF THE PHASE-1 SLICE TREE, and it gates ONLY content that did not exist before tonight.** Six techs: Electrification (poles + burner generator), Electric Smelting, Metallurgy (helm + boots), Plate Armour (cuirass + greaves), Flight Autopilot (DW-29) and Cinderite Refining (GP-2). | Two calls, both load-bearing. **(1) A second tree** because section B.2 gates `types::Smelter`, `types::Belt` and the Ferrite recipes, none of which the web client uses, so wiring it in verbatim gives a player a tech tree that unlocks nothing they can see, which is a menu. The two id blocks are disjoint and a test asserts it, so a save carrying one can never be read as the other. **(2) Only new content** because gating anything already shipped would silently lock a dozen green probes out of the machines they place, and GP-44's rule means adding an existing item to a tech is a ONE LINE change the day that is wanted. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-46 | **A REFUSAL CROSSES THE BRIDGE AS A CODE PLUS THE OFFENDING ID, NEVER AS A SENTENCE.** `ResearchStatus{block, prereq, milestone, item, shortBy}` over `ResearchBlock{None, UnknownTech, AlreadyUnlocked, PrereqMissing, MilestoneMissing, CostShort}`; the client composes the line in `ProgressViews.reasonFor` from names it already holds. | Two things fall out and the second is the one that matters. `/core` has no item names (they live in the SliceRegistry, which `ResearchState` deliberately does not hold), so a sentence would need a second copy of the name table inside the shim. And **the refusal becomes testable**: "refused because the prereq is missing" and "refused because the science is short" are different assertions, a boolean cannot tell them apart, and a gate that refuses for the wrong reason passes a boolean test every single time. The browser probe asserts the refusal MOVES from the prereq to the cost the moment the prereq is bought, which is a claim no boolean can express. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-47 | **DW-29's AUTOPILOT IS GATED ON A MILESTONE: a thing the player DID, as a third kind of precondition beside prereqs and cost.** `MilestoneId`, `TechDef::requiresMilestone`, `ResearchState::setMilestone/hasMilestone`, `milestones::ReachedOrbit`. That is neither a science cost nor a prereq tech: it is a fact about the save, and modelling it as either would let it be faked as either. `kNoMilestone` is the default and `hasMilestone(kNoMilestone)` is true, so every existing tech and every existing test is untouched by the new field. **The separate save list is the important half**: a load path that restored milestones with the techs would hand the autopilot to anybody who pressed F5, and they travel apart precisely so that cannot happen. | GP-48 | **`ModeRules.researchGated` is a THIRD named question, not a reuse of `freeBuild`.** Both answer `!sandbox` today. | This is the branch GP-29's own comment predicted ("research does not exist here yet ... `freeBuild` gives the right answer to a branch written next month"), written the day research landed rather than by somebody remembering to add an or-clause to it. It is a separate question because the two are about different things and will diverge: `freeBuild` is about COST and this is about AVAILABILITY, and the first mode that wants everything unlocked but still paid for (a scenario starting mid-tree, a creative-but-honest mode) needs them apart. GP-29's whole argument is that two questions with the same answer today are still two questions. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-51 | **A HAND CRAFT IS ALL-OR-NOTHING IN BOTH DIRECTIONS: it is REFUSED when the output would not fit, and the refusal crosses the bridge as a CODE.** `gameplay.h` §S.3 gains `CraftBlock{None, NoRecipe, InputsShort, PackFull}` and `HandCrafter::craftBlock`; `canCraft` stays INPUTS ONLY and `payInputs` still asks it, because a structure produces no output and a fit test folded into `canCraft` would refuse a foundation because the pack was full of the stone it was about to spend. **The fit test runs on a COPY OF THE REAL PACK THROUGH THE REAL OPERATIONS**, never a model of the stacking rules: the inputs are removed FIRST, which frees slots, so a pack of twenty full slots whose last slot holds exactly the 5 Wood a furnace costs HAS room for the furnace, and a free-slot count taken before the spend says it does not. `of_gp_craft_block` at **ABI 10**; the client composes the sentence in `GameCore.craftBlockText` and the panel row carries `blockedBy`. **Measured in survival, driven: nine pickaxes crafted, every one with a slot free; at 20 of 20 slots the tenth is refused with `PackFull` while `canCraft` still reads TRUE and Wood 401 / Raw iron 531 are untouched; a forced click through the disabled button pays nothing and flashes "pack is full"; and three recipes read `PackFull` while ten read `InputsShort` in the SAME frame.** | The shipped code spent every input, called `inv.add`, DISCARDED the overflow and returned true, with a comment reasoning that tools and structures stack small so it was "effectively never hit in the slice". The slice then grew a science pack that a player crafts a dozen times with a pack full of ore. **The only symptom is that nothing happens**: the materials are gone, the item never existed, and every instrument reads success. That is standing rule 11's own failure class, an operation reporting an outcome it did not achieve, and it cost the progression lane an hour. The code rather than a boolean is GP-46's argument in a second place: "go and mine" and "go and drop something" are opposite actions, and a boolean throws the difference away. The negative control that makes the /core test mean anything is on the SAME pack with ONE slot different: emptying one stack of coal changes nothing about the inputs and everything about the room, and the identical craft then succeeds and charges exactly its bill. Reverting the fix fails exactly five checks by name, and the one that does NOT fail is `inv.count(PrimitiveFurnace) == 0`, because the old code really did drop the furnace. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-52 | **THE TIER-1 SUIT COSTS 10.6% OF WALKING SPEED (0.8940393), NOT 12% (0.892).** The four shipped multipliers 0.99, 0.95, 0.97, 0.98 multiply to 0.8940393; `progression.h`'s comment and GP-42 both published a transcription of that table which had drifted from it. The table was always right and is untouched. **The property assertion STAYS and the published number is what moves**, in both places it is checked: `probes/equip.js` equips one piece at a time and differences, and `progression_tests` now pins the multiply AGAINST the add. | Worth a decision rather than a one-word edit because of HOW it was caught and how nearly it was not. The additive answer, 1 minus the sum of the encumbrances, is **0.89**, which is near enough to the wrong published figure to look right in a menu and in a review; asserting the published constant would have meant tuning the constant until it passed, which is standing rule 11's exact failure. So the /core test now asserts that the product and the additive answer DIFFER by more than 1e-3 and that the product is the larger, which is the property the code actually claims (compounding is gentler than adding) and which no transcription can satisfy by accident. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-49 | **A MACHINE PLACED WHERE A NEIGHBOUR ALREADY STANDS IS PUT ONE METRE ABOVE IT RATHER THAN REFUSED, and can then never be aimed at again.** Measured: a generator at (x, 21103.98, z) and an electric smelter placed on a bearing 15 degrees away landed at (x, **21104.98**, z), the same tangent position exactly 1.00 m up. NOT FIXED tonight; recorded with the number. | `MachineAddr.u` carries the aim's own height so a furnace aimed at the ground floor of a two-storey base lands on the ground floor (GP-39), and that is right. The side effect is that an aim which stops on a 2 m machine's TOP FACE addresses the cell above it, and the placement then succeeds. The result is a machine floating a metre up that a downward aim ray passes underneath, so it can never be interacted with, demolished or fed, and the only symptom is that E does nothing. The fix is a rule in `MachinePlacement` that an occupied COLUMN refuses rather than stacks, which is a placement-lane change with its own acceptance and not a 4 a.m. edit at the end of an ABI bump. **FIXED 2026-07-27, AND THE DIAGNOSIS ABOVE IS HALF WRONG, which is the part worth keeping.** The probe that reproduced it decomposed the offset into the site's own UP and TANGENT rather than trusting a world axis, and measured: generator at `m1:1,1`, electric smelter at `m1:1,2`, **total 1.0000 m, tangent 1.0000 m, up 8.3e-7 m**. It was never in the air. At a 600 km radius the world y axis is very nearly TANGENTIAL at that site, so a 1.00 m step sideways moved y by 1.00 m (the world dy across the step was in fact 0.025 m) and a lane reading world coordinates called it a metre of altitude. **The consequence is identical and is what makes it worth a refusal**: `FOOTPRINT` is 2 for a generator, a smelter and a drill while the occupancy key is one 1 m cell, so two 2 m machines one cell apart interpenetrate, and `Factory.pick` resolves every bearing that reaches either to whichever is better centred, so one of the two can never again be aimed at, opened, fed or demolished. It was paid for and it is gone, which is worse than a refusal. THE RULE: `machineClash` refuses a placement whose footprint would overlap a neighbour's, `2*|di| < fa + fb` on both site axes, **only between things two cells wide** (a belt is one cell and must be able to run INTO a smelter, which is GP-50's own conclusion), and the ghost NAMES what it is too close to before the button is pressed. **Measured after: the ghost reads `too close to #1 generator`, `ok:false`, the placement count does not move, the refusal is counted, and the negative control puts the SAME smelter from the SAME hand down 2.2361 m away.** | Accepted, FIXED | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-50 | **A WORKING GRID IS A CLUSTER ABOUT TWO METRES ACROSS, so a crosshair cannot address one machine inside it, and hand-feeding is therefore a bootstrap rather than a mechanic.** A small pole supplies 2.5 m (power.h's own ladder), a machine is 2 m on a 1 m grid, and `Factory.pick` resolves an aim to the BEST-CENTRED building within 3.5 m, so every bearing that reaches a smelter also reaches the pole and the generator beside it. | Recorded because it is why the four-smelter deficit case could not be driven in the browser tonight, and because the conclusion is a design one rather than a probe one: **a belt is not a convenience, it is the only way to address a dense base at all.** E-loads-ore exists so a first grid can be started before any belt is laid, and it is deliberately 20 units a press (10 seconds of work at 30 ticks a unit) rather than 5, because 5 units is half a second and a player would spend the whole game pressing E. The next step is a belt into an electric smelter, at which point hand-feeding becomes what it should be: the thing you do once. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-53 | **THE STRUCTURAL FLOOR QUERY ANSWERS WITH THE DECK'S OWN TOP FACE, SOLVED EXACTLY, AND MAY LIFT THE FEET BY ONE STEP TO SEAT THEM ON A FLOOR THEY ARE STANDING INSIDE.** `StructureBodies.deckUnder(dir, rFrom, searchM, riseM)` was a 0.05 m march DOWN from the feet clamped to `Math.min(rFrom, r + 0.05)`, which made it **a function of where the feet were instead of a property of the structure**. It is now a slab intersection of the radial with each near box in that box's own frame: a rotation preserves length, so the line's parameter IS the radius and the slab EXIT is the top face, with no march step and no quantisation. `riseM` is the caller's own `STRUCTURE_STEP_UP_M[0]` (0.55 m), which the shipped 0.50 m deck fits inside and a 4.00 m wall does not. **Measured before, with the player stationary, grounded, 0.00 m/s, on a deck he had walked onto: standing spread 0.198989 m over 300 ticks, 4 snap-ups at a mean period of 74.3 ticks, biggest snap 0.197317 m, feet up to 0.149774 m below the deck's own top face, sinking 0.002683 m EVERY tick (0.161 m/s, exactly one tick of gravity). After: spread 0.000000, snap-ups 0, sink 0.000000.** Second half, from the same probe: a foundation laid on the cell the player is standing in put its 0.50 m slab entirely ABOVE the feet, and a downward-only march never saw it, so **the port answered nothing on 120 of 120 ticks and the player stood 0.499993 m below their own first foundation for ever**; now 120 of 120 and 0 m. `probes/decksink.js`, 12 assertions; reverting only the ANSWER fails 8 by name and leaves both negative controls and the registration check standing. | **Admin's hypothesis was that the `deck > groundR` comparison lost to terrain across a partly buried footprint. It is refuted by measurement: the terrain won that comparison on 0 of 300 ticks.** The comparison is a `max` of two floors and is correct. What was wrong was the deck's own answer. The 1 m to 4 m module move IS implicated, but one step further back than the hypothesis: at a 1 m module a deck was rarely both under you and thicker than the capsule's blind band, and DW-33's fitted plane now deliberately puts a float side under part of every footprint, so on the flat spawn a foundation's slab sits entirely between the ground and 0.50 m and both failure modes became reachable on the first press. The general form is worth more than either: **a floor query that clamps its answer to the querier's own position ratifies the querier's error instead of correcting it**, and gravity supplies a fresh error every tick. Exactness rather than a finer march because a march resolution is a tuned number and a slab test is not: the fix has no threshold in it at all except the step, which is read from the walker's published ladder. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-54 | **A PANEL'S OWN ACTIONS ARE THE PANEL'S, NOT THE UI'S: `Input.setUiCapture(on, alsoAllow)` takes a per-panel allowance, and the assembly bay passes `['board']`.** Reid: "I built a rocket but pressing G in the VAB doesnt do anything." He was right twice over: `board` is not on `UI_ALLOWED` so the press never arrived, and `Systems.ts` discarded it a second time with an explicit `s.vab?.open !== true`. Both are gone. The bay's launch key and a new **Roll out `G`** button in its own bottom bar both run one method, `FlightMode.fromBay(leaveBay)`, which leaves the bay and rolls out, or refuses OUT LOUD while aboard **without closing the bay**. `probes/rollout.js`; reverting only the allowance fails 5 checks by name. | **`board` was NOT added to `UI_ALLOWED`, and that is the decision.** That list is GLOBAL, so `board` on it would also fire from the inventory, the research tree and the power panel, where G would either plant a rocket behind the player or strap them into one from inside a menu. The list's shortness is load-bearing (a click on Craft must not swing a pickaxe) and the fix must not spend it. The three entries already on it are all really "the key that opened this panel has to close it", which is a per-panel rule wearing a global coat; the second argument makes that rule sayable. **The probe's negative control is the assertion that matters**: with the pack open the same key still does nothing, `0 rollouts` and `aboard false`, which is what distinguishes this fix from the wrong one. The refusal not closing the bay came out of `probes/flightabuse.js` going red: shutting a screen to deliver a message puts the player somewhere they did not ask to be. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-55 | **RIGHT-CLICK IN THE ASSEMBLY BAY REMOVES THE PART UNDER THE CURSOR WHEN THE HAND IS EMPTY, AND DROPS THE PART WHEN IT IS NOT.** Reid: "i should be able to remove components i have placed in the VAB. I shouldnt have to clear and start over." `Vab.removeAt` was finished at every layer, refunds included, with **no key and no control**; the only delete in the panel is `design-del`, which throws away a saved DESIGN and is what a player hunting for this would find first. **Measured: 3 parts to 0 on one right-click at the root, `removed` +3, which is `_of_vs_remove`'s documented subtree contract; and two negative controls, a right-click with a part IN HAND drops the hand and removes nothing, and a right-click on empty space removes nothing and SAYS what the button is for.** | One button and two meanings decided by WHAT IS IN YOUR HAND is GP-26's rule for the left button on foot, applied to the right button in the bay, so the bay needs no new binding and no new teaching. Right-click rather than Delete-on-a-selection because it needs no selection step, so removal is one action and not two, and because `demolish` is ALREADY `Mouse2` on foot: the same button means "take that away" in both halves of the game. The empty-space miss speaking rather than doing nothing is the point of the whole change, not politeness: a silent no-op is what taught the player the feature did not exist. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-56 | **THE PROJECT HAS NO GATE THAT ASKS WHETHER A HUMAN CAN REACH A SHIPPED CAPABILITY, and three of Reid's complaints in one session were exactly that.** MEASURED, not asserted. (a) All **54 of 54** actions in `BINDINGS` have a consumer somewhere in `src`, **including `board`**, so the obvious static check would have caught none of the three. (b) **43 of 283** `_of_*` bridge symbols are never named outside `src/sim/wasm`. (c) The sharpest one: sweeping every public method in `src/game`, `src/ui` and `src/player` for callers, **`Vab.removeAt` shows up as reachable ONLY from `src/app/Debug*.ts`** · the debug surface was its only caller, so "has a caller" would have passed on it. Recommended shape in §7. | The three are one family and it is standing rule 11's, aimed at the user-facing surface instead of at code: **a check that passes on something it never examined.** Every gate here proves code is correct, deterministic, fast and in budget; none asks whether a player can get to it. The measurement says a purely static gate can only catch one of the three families (published-and-never-called, which is `AutoLine.lineItems` and `removeAt`), that it MUST exclude the debug surface from the caller set or it scores its own scaffolding as reachability, and that the other two families (a verb muted by a runtime gate; a feature nothing on screen names) need a DRIVEN check and a content one. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-61 | **WITH THE BARE HAND, LEFT CLICK ON A MACHINE OPENS ITS SCREEN, and ONE panel serves both machine families.** Reid: "similar to factorio i should be able to left click on a smelter and have it open up a menu." The rule is placed INSIDE `GameplayInput.world`'s existing bare-hand branch, after `stepBuild` and after the swing's own guard, so GP-26's contract is untouched: a part in hand still places at a machine, a tool still swings at a node, an empty slot still does nothing. There is no ambiguity to resolve because a machine is not harvestable. E is unchanged and still opens, and still closes with `closeWithInteract`. `game/MachineScreen.ts` is new and holds the whole difference between the two families: the hand furnace is a `survival::Furnace` behind a WASM handle (GP-19) and a drill, smelter, electric smelter or generator is a factory-sim citizen behind a build index, and they answer the same three questions (what is in you, what have you made, how far along is this unit) through different exports. `OPENABLE` deliberately excludes a belt (E takes one item off it, FS-28) and a pole (it holds nothing). | **The negative control found a second defect nobody had reported.** Reverting only the one line fails three checks by name, and the third is `opening dug nothing: 0 -> 11`: before this, a bare-hand click at a machine fell through to the swing, missed (a machine is not a node), and the dig action then cratered **11 voxel cells** in the ground under the machine the player was trying to use. Opening flips `uiOpen` before `Systems` reads `digAllowed`, so the same press can no longer do both. That is worth more than the feature's own assertion, because it is the failure that had no complaint attached to it. ONE panel rather than two because the alternative is two screens that drift: the furnace already had one and a factory smelter had none, and a second panel written next month would have got the progress bar from a different place, which is exactly how GP-59's defect class is born. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-62 | **THE SIM DECIDES WHAT A MACHINE ACCEPTS, and the ore side already asks it; the fuel side and the take-ore verb are PUBLISHED SEAMS that say so out loud.** The hand-rolled `[rawIron, rawCopper, coal, wood]` table in `GameplayViews.furnaceView` is gone. The ore half now asks `of_gp_smelt_output_for`, which is /core's own `smeltOutputFor` and the same export the auto-line reads, so the panel cannot offer a smelt the sim will not perform. TWO seams are named rather than filled: **(a)** `sv::fuelTicksPerUnit` has no `of_gp_` query, so `fuelSeam()` lists the two fuels and is documented as deciding only which buttons are OFFERED, never what is ACCEPTED, because the click goes through `of_gp_furnace_insert` and the sim's refusal is the whole content of the sentence; **(b)** `Furnace` has `loadOre` and no `takeOre`, so `GameCore.furnaceTakeOre` feature-detects `of_gp_furnace_take_ore` and returns null, the input cell renders DISABLED with the reason as its tooltip, and `screen.canTakeInput` reports it. | A parallel lane is fixing the sim so a smelter refuses items its recipe does not consume (coal was being smelted into iron), and the one thing that would waste that fix is a second acceptance table in the client that has to be found and edited too. So the panel holds NO opinion it can be wrong about: it asks, and where it cannot ask yet it says which export it is waiting for. The disabled-with-a-reason input cell rather than an absent one is GP-56's finding applied the day it was made: a control that exists and names its blocker is a promise, and a missing control is a feature nobody can find. **What this lane needs:** `of_gp_furnace_take_ore(f, want) -> moved`, and if the typed-acceptance lane routes fuel through the recipe table, one `of_gp_` query for "is this item fuel and for how many ticks" so `fuelSeam` can be deleted rather than maintained. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-63 | **THE PROGRESS BAR IS THE SIM'S OWN COUNTER, MEASURED AT THE PIXEL: worst \|drawn - sim\| = 0 points over 181 samples.** Two counters already existed and neither was on screen for the machine the player was looking at: `of_gp_furnace_state`'s `progress / ticksPerSmelt` for the hand furnace, and `of_net_progress01` (/core's own fixed-point work counter, normalised) for a factory machine. No export was needed and **no ABI bump was taken**. `FurnacePanel.barPct` reads the width back OFF THE ELEMENT and the report carries it beside the view's number, so the probe compares the PAINTED bar with the sim rather than comparing the client with a second copy of its own arithmetic. | The claim is about what the player SEES, so the measurement has to be taken there: a probe that recomputed the percentage would have agreed with itself while the bar on screen sat at zero. **"Reaches 100% once per unit" is asserted as the property it really is, and the reason is in the sim**: `Furnace::tick` completes a unit and zeroes `progress_` in the SAME tick, so a state with `progress == 180` exists for no observer and a literal-100 assertion could only ever be satisfied by a client that faked it. What is asserted instead is that the bar SWEEPS to its top and RESETS exactly once per unit produced (3 sweeps, 3 units, peak 99%), that it equals the sim on every sample in between, and that the sim spent the AUTHORED tick budget doing it (526 ticks against 180 x 3 less the 14 already made). The factory half is proven separately over its own export, because sharing a panel is not sharing a counter: smelter opened, peak 97%, worst error 0. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-64 | **ROUTINE PRODUCTION NO LONGER THROWS A SCREEN-WIDE FLASH. `Feedback.ingot` loses its banner and keeps its chips and its chime, at the machine.** Reid: "the pop up on my screen that +1 Iron is ready should not be a thing or follow me around as im not in the smelter." **THE PATH WAS CHECKED BEFORE ANYTHING WAS DELETED, and it carries four things:** boot progress (`app/Boot.ts`, five call sites), the FELLED moment (`Feedback.felled`), an objective completion (`Objectives.stepGoals`) and the ingot. Only the ingot is routine: the other three are one-time and player-caused, which is what a screen-wide flash is for, and all three still fire. `Objectives.ts`'s own comment claimed the checklist "gets the SAME banner a finished ingot gets" and was corrected in the same commit rather than left to rot. | The complaint is about REPETITION and PLACE, not about feedback: a smelter finishes a unit every three seconds for the rest of the game, so the one cue that must not be global is exactly the one that was. The cue is MOVED rather than removed, which is what the third assertion protects: `ingotCues` still rises once per unit, so a build that had simply stopped noticing smelts would fail even though it would satisfy "no banners" perfectly. The ledger is taken over the WHOLE probe rather than the smelting window, and that was a real correction: the first version measured only the run, reported `legitimatePathExercised: false`, and would have shipped a probe that proved the deletion and not the survival. It now asserts **banners == objectives == 1 across the entire session while 3 ingots were produced**, which says in one line that the channel still works and that no ingot used it. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-16 | **Research / tech tree is a header-only additive layer `core/include/of/research.h`, consuming `gameplay.h` verbatim.** `TechId` (u16, opaque, stable, mirrors GP-13) + `TechDef{id,name,prereqs,cost,unlockRecipes,unlockEntities}` as a DATA `TechTree` (GP-12); `ResearchState` flips unlock bits the build/recipe UX gates on (`isRecipeUnlocked`/`isEntityUnlocked`). **Science is just `ItemStack`s in an `Inventory` pool** (no new mechanism · the factory produces science like any item); science packs append in the SAME `ItemId` space at `0x0020+` (C-3, never renumbering the §7.1 slice block) via the one additive `SliceRegistry::registerItem` extension point. The OFF-WORLD GATE (GP-2) is a tech whose `cost` names a Cinderite-derived science pack · pure data, no special-case code. | Mirrors the Wave-0 cores' thin-layer-over-closed-substrate pattern; keeps the pinned slice content & id space untouched; tech/science are data so balance iterates without code (GP-12); the gate is a cost row, not a branch | Accepted | 2026-06-15 |
| GP-57 | **THE LAUNCH PAD IS THE FIFTH `survival::StructureKind` IN /core AND IS NOT A FIFTH `StructureKind` IN THE CLIENT.** /core's enum is a COST-AND-IDENTITY TAG: `StructureDef` is {kind, item, typeId, name, cost} and carries no geometry, no address, no footprint and no tiling rule, so the only question membership asks is the one §S.6's own header already poses (does it tick, hold ports, draw power, carry an inventory?) and a pad answers no to all four exactly as a foundation does. Its reward for joining is the ENTIRE `of_gp_structure_*` bridge: count, info, can_afford and pay are indexed reads over `structureDefs()`, so a fifth row reached the browser **with NO ABI CHANGE AT ALL** (verified in the running client: `kind 4, item 68, type 68, cost 60 Iron + 120 Stone + 20 Copper`), with one cost authority instead of two. The CLIENT's `StructureKind` is a different thing wearing the same four names: it is the 4 m tiling module, and `isDeck`, `addressAt`, `anchorOf`, `footprintOf`, `supported` and `addrKey` all ask questions a 24 x 24 m monolith cannot answer, so a fifth member there would be six branches saying "not this one". The two enums are joined by `StructureDef.kind` as a FIELD (`find(d => d.kind === PAD_KIND)`) and never by array position. Ids: item `0x0044`, TypeId `0x44`, deliberately below `0x0050` where GP-31's vessel part items live. Tech `LaunchFacilities = 0x0016`, prereq Electrification, 20 Automation + 12 Logistic science, and NO milestone (gating the pad on having reached orbit would be a cycle, because reaching orbit is what the pad is for; the milestone stays on `FlightAutopilot`, the far end of the same arc). | This is the same argument §S.6 already makes for keeping a foundation out of `automation.h`'s `BuildKind`, applied one level down, and the fact that it lands on OPPOSITE sides in the two layers is the point rather than an inconsistency: an enum should be joined when it is a tag and split when it is a dispatch. Getting it right in /core is what made the whole feature an additive header change plus a client module, with no ABI bump to make atomic and no save-version bump to refuse every existing world. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-58 | **A LAUNCH PAD IS FOUNDED ON A 6 x 6 BLOCK OF DECKS AND NEVER ON SOIL, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS WHAT DECIDED IT.** `probes/padground.js`, over the SAME 81 origins out to 6.4 km that GP-36 scored, in one run: a 24 x 24 m footprint judged as ONE plane under DW-33's fitted budget is accepted at **3.7%** (3 of 81), median spread **6.956 m** against a FLOAT + BURY budget of **1.40 m**, p95 **17.5 m**. The same terrain, same run, gives a 4 m deck **59.3%**, which reproduces GP-36's published 58.0% and is what says the instrument is sound rather than the answer convenient. The same ground judged CELL BY CELL as 36 independent 4 m decks gives **50.6%**. So a pad is not a bigger foundation and could not have been made one by widening a tolerance: admitting a 6.96 m median spread into a rule written for 1.40 m is not a tolerance, it is the absence of one. The pad's base plane is therefore the DECK's top face, the terrain question is asked 36 times at the scale DW-33 was actually measured at with GP-38's cantilever available, and `LaunchPadPlacement` contains no ground check at all. The refusal COUNTS: "11 of 36 cells have no foundation (a launch pad rests on a 6 x 6 platform, never on soil)". Also measured and recorded: a corners-only sample (what `footprintOf` gives a deck) accepts **one origin the 5 x 5 grid refuses**, so a 24 m part judged like a 4 m one would have silently accepted ground it should not. | It is DW-29's gate made literal and load-bearing rather than declarative: 36 foundations is 1,440 Stone before the pad's own 60 Iron is paid, so the rocket programme cannot start until the base exists, which is exactly the tie between the two halves of the game DW-29 says the gate is for. It also reads right, because a real pad is laid on prepared concrete. And it is the third time in this domain that re-measuring at a new scale overturned a constant that had been fine at the old one (GP-23, GP-36, now this), which is why the measurement is quoted here rather than the conclusion alone. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-59 | **ONE CLICK PLACES ONE PART, AND THE RULE IS "HAS THE PLAYER MOVED?", NOT "HAS THE CURSOR TRAVELLED FAR ENOUGH?"** Reid: "when i click to place a foundation, it typically places multiple." MEASURED FIRST, by sampling the ghost's own cell key every fixed tick through a real `pointerdown`/`pointerup` pair with the mouse held still: the press commits in cell (0,0), the very NEXT tick names (0,-1), and every tick after that names (0,0) again. **The placement causes the excursion**: `aimPoint` marches against what is already BUILT as well as against the ground (it has to, or no upper storey could be aimed at), so the new deck's top face and the walker stepping UP onto it both move the aim in the tick after the commit, with the player never touching the mouse. A human click is 60 to 150 ms, 4 to 9 ticks at 60 Hz, so it covers that tick essentially always. **THREE FIXES WERE TRIED AND THE FIRST TWO ARE RECORDED BECAUSE THEY FAILED HONESTLY.** (1) Locking the drag to the plane of its first part: rejected, because the press resolves against the GROUND and the lock resolves against the fitted PLANE, and those differ by up to the float budget, so the lock ITSELF caused a one-cell jump. (2) Requiring a cell to be named on two consecutive ticks: better, but a 60 ms click still laid two, because the excursion is sometimes TWO ticks; lengthening it would be choosing a number to sit above however long the transient happens to be today. (3) SHIPPED: a held button places again only once the player has supplied INPUT, which is the observer's own yaw and pitch changing OR a movement key being down. Neither responds to the world moving under a stationary player, and there is no threshold anywhere in it. The settle test from (2) is kept as well, because it is free and defends the same seam from the other side. **Measured after, with real PointerEvents at 60, 90, 120, 150 and 100 ms: 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, with the gate firing 3, 8, 14, 22 and 27 times; a click made WHILE WALKING lays at most 1; and the CONTROL, a genuine drag sweeping the crosshair, still lays a run.** THE COST, stated: holding the button and walking forward without turning no longer extends a STRUCTURAL run, which is the rule belts already follow (FS-26). | The failed attempts are the valuable part. Both would have passed a probe that only clicked once, and (2) passed four of the five durations, which is exactly what a tuned threshold looks like from the inside. What separated them is that the shipped rule names the actual distinction between a click and a drag (the player did something) instead of measuring a proxy for it (the cursor went far enough), and a rule with no number in it cannot drift when the module, the pitch or the walker's step height changes. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-60 | **A STRUCTURAL CELL KEY CARRIES ITS SITE ID, AND IT USED NOT TO.** `addrKey(siteId, addr)` gives `d:<site>:<i>,<j>,<level>`, plus `deckKey`/`wallKey` for the callers that hold coordinates rather than an `Addr`. Every site numbers its own cells from (0,0) and the occupancy map is ONE map, so a base founded 100 m away had a cell (0,0) too and `s.has(key)` answered **"already built here" for a cell in a site nothing had ever been built in**. The SUPPORT checks were already site-aware (`hasDeck` compared `siteId` after the lookup), so this was the one question in the set asked in the wrong space, which is exactly why it survived: every positive test in the suite builds ONE base. A restore now REBUILDS the key from the site and the address rather than reading the saved one, so a slot written before tonight cannot carry the collision forward. | Found by a probe doing the right thing for an unrelated reason: `probes/clickonce.js` teleports 60 m between measured clicks so a skipped row cannot hide a double placement, and it was told the fresh ground was already built on. The general form is worth keeping: **a key that omits the frame it is relative to is correct exactly until there are two frames**, and a suite that only ever builds one base can never reach the second one. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-65 | **EVERY PLACED THING HAS HEALTH, AS PURE DATA, BEFORE ANYTHING CAN DAMAGE IT, AND IT IS ONE BOOK KEYED BY THE IDENTITY EACH POPULATION ALREADY PERSISTS.** `web/src/game/Health.ts` holds the catalogue (four tables plus an authored-but-uninstanced vessel table) and the verbs; `web/src/game/HealthCensus.ts` holds the only code that knows what a population is. **The obvious design is an `hp` field on `Placed`, `StructurePart`, `Machine` and `PadPart`, and it is the design this would have if it could reach every population. It cannot:** the launch pad lives in `LaunchPad*.ts` and a vessel part in `Vab*.ts`, both owned by other live lanes tonight, so a per-row field would have covered three populations out of five and left the launch pad, the most valuable building in the game, immortal. That is precisely the silent gap the work exists to close. One book covers all of them, has one save path and one restore path. **The price is paid explicitly rather than assumed away:** a side table can drift from the population it describes, so `reconcile` DERIVES the book from the four lists on every fixed tick (no `register` call at any spawn site to forget, the same argument GP-25 made about Escape handlers and GP-38 about support depth) and `audit` independently compares the book against the live count of every population. `missing` and `stale` are published in `__of.game().health` and a probe holds both at zero. **Keys:** `s:` a `StructurePart.key` (already site-qualified, GP-60), `f:` a `Placed.cell` (persisted verbatim, GP-27), `p:` a pad's site and block address, and `m:` a hand machine's position on a centimetre grid, DERIVED because a machine carries no id that survives a reload (its /core handle is re-created by `furnaceCreate` on every spawn and its array index shifts on the first demolition) while its position is written and handed back verbatim. **Only the WOUNDED are saved**, so an unattacked world costs two bytes and anything in the list is by construction damaged; the CEILING is not saved, because it is catalogue data and re-reading it is what lets a rebalance reach worlds that already exist. `SAVE_VERSION` deliberately does not move, the same rule `discovery`, `pads` and `progress` were added under. **MEASURED THROUGH A REAL BROWSER RELOAD** (`reload.mjs --setup=probes/damagesave.js`, one context so IndexedDB is the store a player has): a belt at **57/80**, a foundation at **363/500** and a hand furnace at **259/300** were left damaged, the page was reloaded, and all three came back at those exact numbers with **0 orphans, 0 missing, 0 stale, 0 unknown kinds, 3 wounded of 5 tracked before and after**. **THE NEGATIVE CONTROL IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING:** reverting the single line `health: health.serialize()` to `health: []` fails **five** checks by name, three of them reading `came back ABSENT`, and the in-page `of.save()`/`of.load()` round trip passes that same nobbled build, which is exactly why only this runner can ask the question. Health lands with **no damage source anywhere in the client**; `__of.damage({key, amount})` is the same `HealthBook.damage` a weapon will call, with the trigger left off. **The trigger was pulled at GP-91 and this file did not have to change**: `EnemySwarm` calls `damage(key, dps * dt)` and the reload proof written here already covered it. | Retrofitting a health field onto entities after combat exists means touching every population, every save row and every restore path at the same time as the thing that made the bug visible. Doing it first is a small, boring, provable change; doing it second is the expensive mistake. The catalogue is scaled against `enemies.h`'s own rows rather than invented, so the numbers mean something the moment an attacker exists: a Skitterer at 7 dps and 1.5 m reach holds against a 350 hp wall for 50 seconds and a pack of ten for 5. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-85 | **THE ENEMY LOOP CROSSES THE BRIDGE AT ABI 15, AND THE SEAM IS `enemies.h`'S OWN LINE RATHER THAN ONE DRAWN HERE.** `web/wasm/of_enemies_api.inc` (§20) exports emitters in, the pollution field's spread and decay, nest absorption and attribution, the three separately accounted evolution terms, and `AttackWave`s out with an origin, a target and a roster; plus the two callbacks the combat lane owes back (`of_en_damage_nest`, `of_en_destroy_nest`), persistence, and DW-28's four counters. It does NOT export pathfinding, movement, combat resolution or anything rendered, because that header says out loud that it does not own them: the CLIENT spawns, paths, draws and kills the individual creatures. **PURELY ADDITIVE:** not one existing export changed name, signature or scratch layout, the `expected.json` diff is a single deliberate line, and the bump exists only so the handshake can say the surface is there. **The catalogue crosses through `of_en_type` so the client NEVER authors an enemy's numbers**, because a TypeScript table of those five rows would be a second balance authority a designer editing `enemies.h` could not reach. A wave is TWO calls with fixed strides rather than one with a variable stride, because a roster is variable-length and a flat array with a variable stride is the shape that gets mis-indexed. **MEASURED:** `parity.mjs` at abi=15 gives TIER 0 self-determinism **126 passed 0 failed** and TIER A cross-toolchain exact **108 passed 0 failed**, both gating, matching the map lane's ABI 14 run exactly; TIER B's 3 informational libm failures are the pre-existing documented tan/asin delta. New `web/wasm/test/enemyloop.mjs`: a nest fed by an emitter 900 m away dispatches **1 wave of 50 Skitterers AIMED AT THE EMITTER THAT FED IT**, its target direction within **1e-6 m** of the emitter's; the cloud holds **5,565.9 over 877 cells of 146.484 m**; evolution **0.05149219** decomposes BIT-EXACTLY into time 0.04585530 + pollution 0.00563689 + kills 0; the Skitterer arrives carrying 15 hp, 7 dps, 6 m/s and 1.5 m reach. **THE NEGATIVE CONTROL IS `enemies.h`'S OWN AND IS THE MOST LOAD-BEARING ASSERTION IN THE FILE:** a nest alone in an unpolluted hemisphere dispatches **0 waves over 400 pollution ticks** and absorbs 0, while time alone still moves evolution to 0.087610. A wave timer would pass every positive check and fail this one. Persistence: 35,409 bytes round-trip to a BIT-IDENTICAL `stateHash`, and an empty stream is refused rather than read. **One thing written into the test rather than left to be rediscovered:** restoring into a sim built from a DIFFERENT world seed reproduces every serialised field and still differs in `stateHash`, because the hash covers the construction seed and the stream deliberately does not repeat it. The world already carries the seed. | The bridge was left half-moved by an interrupted pass: source at 15 against a dist answering 14, which is exactly the failure `OF_ABI_VERSION` exists to catch and would have thrown at boot for anybody who started a server from that tree. Standing rule 9 says the bump is atomic, so all six pieces moved in one commit and that commit boots. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-86 | **THE GUN IS HITSCAN, INFINITE-AMMO, AND IT HAS FOUR SIMULTANEOUS TELLS BECAUSE A GUN THAT SILENTLY DECREMENTS A NUMBER FEELS BROKEN EVEN WHEN IT IS CORRECT.** `Weapon.ts` is the rule (cooldown, ray, damage, no renderer, no audio), `WeaponFx.ts` is the pictures, `Gunnery.ts` is the composition and holds `pullTrigger`. **HITSCAN, and the reason is not that it is cheaper.** (1) A projectile is a SECOND PHYSICS MODEL: DW-12 says there is no physics engine, so a bullet would need its own body-frame integration, its own broadphase against moving enemies and its own answer to where the ground is, which is standing rule 1's exact trap. (2) Hitscan CANNOT MISS FOR A REASON THE PLAYER CANNOT SEE, because the shot is the SAME ray the crosshair is built from, so what you are looking at and what you hit are one computation; with a projectile they are two, and the day they disagree the player is told they missed a thing they were looking at. (3) `enemies.h`'s roster runs 3.4 to 6.0 m/s with 1.5 to 12 m reaches, so at a base fight's ranges the lead a projectile would demand is under one frame: not a skill, a latency. (4) The tracer is a RENDERING choice, not a simulation one, so the thing a player actually wants from a bullet is free. **THE FOUR TELLS, each answering a different question and each counted separately:** muzzle FLASH (did my input register), the REPORT (the same answer in a second sense, which is what makes it land at 400 rpm when the flash is under three frames), the TRACER (where did it go), the IMPACT (did it hit). Two new synthesised voices, `shot` (crack + body + tail, the tail being the layer nobody can name and everybody misses) and `impact`, because a pick on stone and a gun going off are different events and a player who cannot tell them apart by ear cannot tell whether they fired. **THE GUN IS THE ELEVENTH HOTBAR SLOT** on `KeyF` with no digit, GP-57's argument verbatim: the bar grows because the game grows, the digit row is full at ten, and a weapon on F is the convention every shooter has taught. It is its own `SlotContent` kind rather than a `PartKind` because it places nothing and ticks nothing: what it changes is what the LEFT BUTTON DOES, which is exactly the question that enum exists to answer (GP-26). **MEASURED** (`probes/gun.js`, frozen static build, build stamp `69c5a75+dirty`): one tap fires exactly **1 round** and produces **+1 flash PAINTED over 2 frames, +1 tracer PAINTED over 3 frames, 1 `shot` voice, 1 `impact` voice, +5 debris and +1 ground hit**; a held trigger for 1.0 s fires **6 rounds at 0.15 s each with 54 cycling refusals COUNTED**, which is the weapon's rate and not the tick rate. **THE NEGATIVE CONTROL IS THE SAME HELD CLICK WITH THE BARE HAND: 0 shots over 30 held ticks.** A build that fired on every click would pass every positive assertion above. **The probe found a real instrumentation defect and it is the durable lesson:** the first version asserted `flashVisible` at a sample, which gives a headless probe three frames to hit and is a flaky test wearing a strict test's clothes; `flashFrames` and `tracerFrames` now rise INSIDE the same branch that sets `visible`, so REQUESTED and PAINTED are two numbers and a gun that fires and shows nothing is a findable state rather than an argument. | Reid asked for a gun with infinite bullets and said what mattered: it has to read as a weapon. The rule and the pictures are separate files so the rule is testable with no renderer and the pictures cannot quietly acquire an opinion about damage. `pullTrigger` is a free function in `Gunnery.ts` rather than a `Gameplay` method because that file is 80 lines over its cap, which is the same call `PersistLedger.ts` was. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-115 | **THE SNAP SEARCH NOW RANKS NEAR MISSES, AND THE REFUSAL NAMES THE FACE IT COULD NOT MATE.** `nearestNodeToRay` filtered candidates through `fitAt` BEFORE ranking them, and that one line is Reid's complaint 1 in full: a node that existed and did not fit was INVISIBLE, `Vab.active` stayed null, the ghost vanished, and the click was refused with "no attachment node there", which is a false statement about a face the player is looking straight at. Worse, it made every sentence `fitAt` composes unreachable, because its only other caller re-tested a node this function had already guaranteed fits, so that branch was provably dead code and "1.25 m will not mate with 2.50 m" had never once appeared on screen. **MEASURED on the shipped build over a 41x41 grid of screen cells: a 1.25 m tank in hand over a bare 2.50 m tank snapped on 0 of 1681 cells**, and the message named neither diameter. `snapAtRay` now returns the best FITTING node and the best node full stop; `Vab` holds the near miss as `blocked`, DRAWS it in red at the place it would have gone (GP-8's colour code reaching the bay at last), and refuses with its own reason. The near-miss pass deliberately prefers faces that carry a class or a rule over the 72-node radial cage, whose refusal is always the same sentence, or the answer would be "Fuel Tank (small) has no radial mount" while the player points at a mating face two centimetres away. The class sentence NAMES THE REMEDY, which is DW-29a's own argument (two diameter classes with no adapter are two disjoint catalogues) finally reaching the player: **"Fuel Tank (small) is 1.25 m and that face is 2.50 m: put a Stack Adapter between them (2.50 m below, 1.25 m above)"**. | The brief's three candidate causes were the large tank's top node not being exposed, the adapter reporting the wrong class on its own top node, and the snap search rejecting a class transition. Measurement acquitted the first two: the L tank publishes exactly one top face at cls 2.50, the adapter presents 2.50 below and 1.25 above, and driven with the adapter in hand the snap answered on 131 of 1681 cells and the three-part stack closed to a measured worst joint gap of 0. The third was right in substance and wrong in blame: the search did not reject the transition, it HID it, and hiding a refusal is worse than making it, because a player cannot tell a rule from a bug. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-116 | **A RADIAL DECOUPLER IS A PYLON AND PUBLISHES ONE OUTWARD NODE.** Reid's complaint 2 was "Things dont snap to the radial decouplers", and the brief correctly said "no nodes authored" and "nodes ignored" are different fixes. **It was the first.** Driven in the shipped bay: place a radial decoupler on a tank and the node list contains **0 entries with the decoupler as parent** (74 nodes, all of them the tank's 1 top, 1 bottom and 72 radial). `vessel.h` gives `DecouplerRadial` `nodeTop = false`, `nodeBottom = false` and `radialMount = true`, and `attachNodes` then denied it a radial face as well through the blanket "a radial part is not itself a radial HOST" rule, leaving a part with nothing on it at all. A booster strapped to the side is the ONLY thing that part exists for, so a radially-attached DECOUPLER, and nothing else, is now a host: exactly ONE node of a new kind `pylon`, outward at its own mount angle, at offset 0 so where you put the pylon is where the booster's base lands. Not the 72-node cage a 0.30 m hull would otherwise grow, and ONE tenant only, because a pylon that kept offering its node would let a second booster occupy the same point in space. The geometry falls out of `vessel.h`'s own `originFrom`: parent radius 0.625 plus pylon radius 0.15 puts the booster's mount plane at **0.775 m**, so a 1.25 m booster stands 0.15 m clear of a 1.25 m core, which is the decoupler's own length. `fitAt` refuses a decoupler on a pylon by name (the accidental tower) and refuses anything with no radial mount by name, which is GP-115's machinery proving the pylon is a rule and not a hole. | The measurement is what made this a two-line fix rather than a hunt. "Nothing snaps to it" was equally consistent with a missing rule and a missing node, and the node census settled it in one run. Choosing `pylon` as its own kind rather than reusing `radial` is what keeps symmetry off it (four copies at four angles around one 0.30 m decoupler is not what a player pressing 4 means) with no special case at the call site. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-117 | **THE PROBE SERVER MERGES THE REAL VITE CONFIG INSTEAD OF RESTATING A SUBSET OF IT.** BT-27 added `define.__OF_BUILD__` to `vite.config.ts` only, so `vite.probe.config.ts` served a bundle in which `__OF_BUILD__` was an undeclared identifier, `HudLines` threw on its first line, and EVERY driven probe on that port failed with 21 page errors before it could measure anything. `mergeConfig(base, ...)`. | A probe server that is a hand-maintained copy of the real one is a second authority, which is the failure this project has paid for repeatedly. | GP-118 | **THE PRE-FLIGHT VERDICT (R10), AND BOTH RULES THE BRIEF NAMED ARE WRONG AS WRITTEN.** `web/src/game/VabCheck.ts` reduces /core's own stage table to `{ok, faults, warnings, liftBurn, liftTwr}` with CODES rather than sentences. The brief asked for "any burn with zero engines that is not the last one, and any first burn with TWR below 1.0", and the probe's own negative control refuted both in one run. **(1) A zero-engine first burn is the NORMAL shape of a staged rocket here.** `vessel.h` documents the KSP sequence it follows: pressing stage k DROPS the spent hardware of burn k-1 and LIGHTS burn k, so `stages[0].decouple` is empty BY CONSTRUCTION, and `of_vs_autostage` giving a leading decoupler group its own burn produces a zero-engine burn 0 on any multi-stage design. GP-73 measured that exact vehicle (pod / tank / engine / decoupler, which GP-32 permits) at burn 0 = 0 engines and burn 1 = TWR 3.7026, AND LIFTING; driven here it reads burn 0 at 0 engines, 0 decouplers, 6820 kg unchanged end to end, against burn 1 at 1 engine and TWR 2.4091. **Reverting to the brief's literal rules refuses that vehicle with three faults including "stage 0 lifts at TWR 0.00"**, which is the cage GP-73 spent a session undoing. **(2) "the first burn" is therefore the wrong burn.** The question is whether the first burn THAT HAS ENGINES can leave the ground, which is now labelled `Lift TWR (s0)` on screen in place of `Pad TWR` (that cell was `stats.padTwr`, stage 0's TWR, which on GP-73's shape reads 0.00 and looks like a dead rocket). Faults: `empty`, `no-engine`, `twr-below-1`, and **`dry-burn`, which the brief did not name and which is the most literal reading of "an empty first stage"**: an engine with no tank in its own stage group has thrust, TWR 13.59 and a burn time of 0.0 s, and a TWR check alone waves it straight through. Warnings: `idle-stage` (a press that lights nothing and drops nothing, GP-73's shape), `coast-stage`, `unstable`, `no-crew`. The stage table gained `decouplers`, read from `_of_vs_stage_info`'s second word, which has been published since W8 and had never been read. | Reid has lost two build cycles to rockets that could not launch, and both facts were already drawn on this screen as figures. The two sharpenings are not taste: each is forced by a measurement this project already owned, and the probe holds both halves at once, so a future simplification back to the brief's wording fails four checks by name. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-119 | **A HOPELESS DESIGN IS REFUSED ONCE AND LAUNCHED ON A SECOND PRESS, AND THE ARM IS BOUND TO THE DESIGN REVISION.** `VabRollOut.ts`, a 6 s window. A check that can be wrong must never be the only authority over whether the player may act; a check that stops an ACCIDENT and yields to an INTENTION stops the accident either way and can never become a cage. **The revision binding was found by the probe, not designed:** it armed the confirm on one hopeless design, cleared the bay, built a second one, and the FIRST press launched it. An override that outlives its subject is not a confirmation, it is a six-second window in which the guard is simply off. | Reid's own words are the test DW-30 sets for a concession: this removes a frustration (a wasted roll-out) without removing a decision, because he may still launch anything he means to. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-120 | **THE PARTS RAIL IS TABBED, AND THE AXIS REID NAMED WAS THE WRONG ONE.** Nine tabs in `GROUPS` order with short labels, one group painted at a time, the tab state living in `VabPanel`, because which page of a list you are on is not a change to the design and a tab press must not autosave a vessel or re-derive a stage table. `revealPart` switches tabs to reach a part that is not on the page you are looking at, because with one tab in the DOM `partButton` answers null for two thirds of the catalogue and a caller that read that as "not offered" would be wrong about a part one press away. **Reid said "i shouldnt have to horizontal scroll" and horizontal was not what was happening.** With the CSS fix removed the list still measured **0 px of sideways overflow at 1600, 900 and 760 px of window**, because its rows are block-level at width 100% inside an `overflow-y: auto` column. What actually overflowed was the other axis: the nine tab lists sum to **6237 px of content in a 693 px rail**. After the tabs, **every tab fits with 0 px of scroll on either axis** in sandbox (24 parts over 9 tabs) and in survival (13 over 7), the tabs partition the catalogue exactly, and the bottom bar still fits after gaining a button. The `overflow-x: hidden` and the flex `min-width: 0` were kept as a guard, and the probe asserts BOTH axes so neither claim can quietly become the other. | Tabs are what he asked for and they are right regardless; the honest part is that the reason he gave for wanting them was not the reason the list was painful. Recorded because a fix aimed at the wrong axis would have passed a probe that only counted tabs. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
