| GP-122 | **BUILDING DOWNWARD ALREADY WORKED; WHAT WAS MISSING WAS THE CAMERA.** Reid's complaint 3 was "i have to start with the engine and build up, but i should be able to drag the whole ship up and place things on the bottom". Measured BEFORE any change: pod as root, then a tank attached `ATTACH_BOTTOM` at y = -4.00 and an engine at y = -5.60, three parts, 8.10 m, pad TWR 3.70, with `_of_vs_attach` doing exactly what KSP does and `VabView.setFloor` sliding the floor down under it, which IS "the whole ship moved up" from the player's side. So the capability was never missing, and the brief's suggested change (translate the stack rather than move the root) was already the shipped behaviour. What was missing is that the camera framed ONCE on entry and never again, so a stack growing downward walked off the bottom of the view and the player had no reason to believe the click had done anything. `VabCamera.keepInView` follows only the TARGET, and only when the assembly has actually left the box that was framed, so a chosen yaw, pitch and zoom survive. | Worth recording as a wrong premise corrected by measurement rather than as a feature. Two of the four complaints in this brief turned out to be about FEEDBACK rather than capability (this one and complaint 4's axis), which is a pattern: the bay tells the player very little about why nothing happened. GP-115's red ghost is the general answer. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-79 | **THE PLAYER HAS HEALTH, A BAR THAT READS IT, AND A DEATH RULE THAT WAS UNSPECIFIED AND IS NOW CHOSEN OUT LOUD.** `web/src/game/PlayerHealth.ts` is the rule and `PlayerVitals.ts` is the composition, split for the same reason `Health.ts` and `HealthCensus.ts` are: the rule takes a `dt` and a list of things hurting you and has no idea a HUD or a body exists. **THE DAMAGE ARITHMETIC KNOWS NOTHING ABOUT AN ENEMY:** a `HurtSource` is a name, a `dps`, a `reachM` and a distance, which are exactly `enemies.h`'s `damagePerSecond` and `reachM` plus a measurement, so when the wave lane hands over a roster the numbers are /core's own and nothing here re-authors them. The list was EMPTY until GP-91 filled it, which was a correct state and not a stub: the regen clock, the death rule, the banner and the respawn all ran against it, driven by `__of.hurt`, which is the same `PlayerHealth.hurt` an enemy in reach now reaches through `step`. **Measured with real attackers: 36 Skitterers in reach at once, 151.2 points taken, one death and one respawn, cause `Skitterer` off /core's own catalogue.** **Reach is a THRESHOLD, not a falloff**, because `enemies.h` says a Lancer's 12 m outranges a short-reach turret, which is a statement about a threshold; a smooth falloff would make every enemy on the planet deal a little damage, which is both wrong and unboundedly expensive. **DEATH AND RESPAWN, THE CHOICE REID DID NOT SPECIFY: 150 hp, no regen for 6 s after a hit then 8 hp/s, and at zero the player DROPS NOTHING, blacks out for 5 s and stands up at the landing site at full health.** The cost of dying is TIME plus whatever the attack did to the base while nobody was defending it, and that second half is real only because building damage now persists (GP-65). Dropping the pack as a corpse was rejected: it needs a corpse entity, a marker, a decay rule, corpse persistence and a corpse-vs-corpse case, all so a loop that already punishes you can punish you twice, and Factorio (the reference this whole feedback loop is copied from) does not do it. No death at all was rejected because it removes the only stake the loop has: if losing a fight costs nothing then the pollution you emit costs nothing and the causal chain stops being a pressure and becomes weather. Respawning at the LANDING SITE rather than at the nearest building is the simple answer AND currently the kind one, because the clearing is grown at the spawn, and the day those stop agreeing is the day to write the rule. **THE BAR SAYS `SAFE` RATHER THAN DISAPPEARING when the player cannot be hurt**, which is `Structures.affordInCore`'s rule applied to the HUD: a mode that lifts a rule must publish the answer it is overriding, or a bar that failed to render and a player who cannot be hurt are the same picture. It is also outside `setVisible`, like the sandbox badge, because the moment you most need to know you are on 12 health is with a panel open in front of you. **MEASURED, and the two runs of one probe are the assertion** (`probes/playerhealth.js`, on a frozen static build because two other lanes were pushing broken modules through the dev server): with `?sandbox=1&combat=1` a 42-damage bite takes 150 to **108**, the DRAWN bar reads **71.998%** off the element's own width against the sim's 72.000% (GP-63's pixel discipline, so the assertion is the painted bar and not a second copy of the client's arithmetic), health holds at **108 through 2.5 s** of a 6 s delay and reaches **129.6 by 8.5 s**, a killing blow kills once, banners the screen, labels the bar `DOWN 4.7s`, refuses to let a dead player build, and the respawn returns full health **0 deg** from the landing site with the pack byte-identical. With `?sandbox=1` alone the SAME probe passes with the OPPOSITE outcome: **2 hurt events counted, 0 damage taken, 0 deaths, label `SAFE`, bar 100%.** Counting the event while taking no damage is the whole point, because `hurtEvents` rising with `totalTaken` at zero is a different picture from a damage path that was never wired, which is both at zero. **ONE REAL BUG FOUND BY THE PROBE AND WORTH KEEPING:** the death banner was latched off a transition `step` happened to witness (`wasDead` read before the tick), so it fired when the killing blow landed INSIDE `step` and silently did nothing when anything else killed the player, which is every direct `hurt` call: a probe's blow today, a fall or a scripted event tomorrow. It is latched off the STATE now, and a latch cannot care who did it. | The brief left death and respawn unspecified and this is the answer, logged rather than buried. Health is 150 rather than 100 because a Skitterer deals 7 dps and a 100 hp player dies to two of them in seven seconds, which is a horror game rather than a factory game. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-87 | **THE SEAM IS `enemies.h`'S OWN LINE, AND THE CLIENT SEEDS THE FIRST NESTS BECAUSE NOBODY ELSE DOES.** `EnemyLoop.ts` drives /core's loop and reads its answers back; `EnemySwarm.ts` owns what the creatures then do; `Enemies.ts` composes them and is the ONE field `Gameplay` holds. /core owns WHY an attack happens and this lane owns everything after the `AttackWave`, which is that header's own published boundary rather than a line drawn here. **Nests are seeded by the CLIENT from the world seed**, four of them on a ring 620 to 1150 m from the spawn, and that is a gap paid for openly rather than a design: `enemies.h` expects generation-0 nests seeded at worldgen and world-gen has no nest pass, so somebody has to place the first ones. The ring is inside the 1,135 m e-folding radius of the cloud on Forge, which is the whole causal chain in one constant: a base that pollutes hard reaches them and one that does not never will. **Measured, seed 0xbf00d01: nest 1 at 707.8 m absorbed 1,059 units while the other three absorbed 341, 277 and 78, and only nest 1 attacked.** The day world-gen grows a POI pass, `seedNests` is deleted and `of_en_add_nest` is called from there. | The alternative was to invent a client-side wave rule, which is the one thing the whole design forbids. Seeding is the smallest thing this lane can own that makes /core's loop reachable, it is deterministic from the seed, and it is one call to delete when the right owner exists. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-88 | **EMITTERS AND BITE TARGETS ARE DERIVED FROM THE LIVE POPULATIONS EVERY POLLUTION WINDOW, NEVER REGISTERED AT A SPAWN SITE.** `EnemyTargets.ts` reads the same four lists `HealthCensus` reads and keys both lists with `Health.ts`'s OWN key functions, so damage cannot land on a key nothing recognises. `enemies.h` section 11 offers the registration shape (mint on build, deactivate on idle, remove on demolish) and it is DECLINED. The rate for a building is asked of `of_en_machine_rate` per row rather than cached in a TypeScript table. **Measured: 6 derived emitters against 6 in /core, 0 refused, every rate 2.0, which is /core's smelter row; and three belts added ZERO emitters**, because /core rates a belt at zero and a client carrying a copy of that table would have passed every other assertion in the run. | This is `HealthCensus.ts`'s argument applied twice more, and it is applied rather than restated because the failure has now happened to this project twice: the seventh place that places a building is written next month, it does not have the call, and NOTHING ANYWHERE SAYS SO. A building with no emitter angers nothing and a building with no target row is invulnerable, and both read exactly like a quiet world. The per-row WASM call is the cheapest possible guarantee that a rebalance in `enemies.h` reaches the game rather than only the header. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-89 | **A CREATURE IS A MARCH, NOT A PATHFINDER**, and its radius is re-read off the LIVE oracle every step. THE PLAYER WINS EVERY TIE. **Measured: 707.8 m to 522.6 m in 30 s = 6.175 m/s against the catalogue's 6.0**, arriving at the emitter that fed the nest and eating it. | DW-12 restated rather than laziness: there is no physics engine and no navmesh, the player is a kinematic capsule resolved against the surface oracle, and a creature is the same idea with a goal. Re-reading the radius every step is GP-28's lesson, because a carried radius is how a creature ends up walking at the height it spawned at across a crater the player dug an hour later. Stopping to attack is Factorio's own behaviour and is what makes a wall worth building without needing steering. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-90 | **THE ENEMY INSTANCE POOL STARTS AT 32, WHICH IS DELIBERATELY BELOW ONE WAVE'S ROSTER, AND EXHAUSTION SHOUTS ON THREE CHANNELS.** The shared `InstancePools.CAPACITY` of 256 is above anything one fight produces, so a pool starting there would never grow and the growth path would never run outside a test written for it. **Measured in the ordinary fight: `grows: 1`, capacity 32 to 64, `refused: 0`, 39 instances for 36 creatures and 3 nests.** **Measured with the ceiling pinned at 32 so it CANNOT grow: `refused: 3960`, `live: 50` creatures alive and biting against 32 drawn, one console error naming the pool, the HUD budget line reading `enemies 32/32  POOL FULL: 3872 NOT DRAWN`, and a screen flash reading `ENEMY POOL FULL: creatures are alive and NOT DRAWN`.** /core's own second ceiling (`of_en_waves_truncated`) is published on the same report line so the two are read together. | DW-28 binding, and the start size is the part worth defending. A pool that never reaches its growth path in normal play is exactly how the 256-machine wall survived: the code past the constant was unreachable, so the first fight big enough to need it was the first time it had ever executed. Starting under one wave puts the doubling on the ordinary path where a probe can assert it, and costs two `setInstanceCount` calls per wave. The screen flash is on top of the shared HUD line because an undrawn building is merely invisible and an undrawn creature is invisible AND biting you. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-91 | **COMBAT RESOLVES BOTH WAYS, AND NEITHER END REIMPLEMENTS A RULE THAT ALREADY EXISTED.** Against the player, the swarm pushes one `HurtSource` per creature in reach and `PlayerHealth.step` spends `dt` against it, so there is still exactly ONE place the player's health changes and ONE place invulnerability is honoured. Against the base it calls `HealthBook.damage(key, dps * dt)` on GP-65's book, which needed no change at all. The gun's `onShotHit` seam, published empty at GP-86, is now filled and nothing in the weapon path learned what an enemy is. **Measured: 36 creatures in reach at once, 151.2 points taken, 150 to 0, cause `Skitterer` at 7 dps off /core's catalogue, one death, one respawn; two smelters chewed to 0/400 and BACK AT 0/400 through a real browser reload (2 applied, 0 orphans, 0 missing, 0 stale, 0 unknown kinds); 14 aimed rounds, 14 hits, 14 kills, 0 ground strikes.** | The order health landed in (GP-65 first, then GP-79, then the gun, then this) is vindicated by how little this step had to touch: the damage arithmetic, the death rule, the reload proof and the audit all existed and all held. Crediting a creature would mean inventing a fourth input in TypeScript, which is a second answer to why the difficulty is rising and the two-authority failure the whole enemy seam is arranged to avoid. It also gives the loop its one real lever: killing a wave saves your base and costs the nest its pollution, and killing the nest is what changes the game. | GP-100 | **ESCAPE OPENS THE GAME MENU, AND "ONLY WHEN NOTHING ELSE IS OPEN" IS NOT A CONDITION ANYBODY WROTE.** Reid: "queue up a menu that i can open when i hit escape (if im not already in another menu)". The parenthesis is satisfied by the two branches that already existed: `ModalStack.closeTop` shuts whatever is on top, the HAND is registered as a modal so a held part is dropped first, and only with both of those exhausted is the fallback reached. The fallback is a HOOK ON THE STACK (`whenNothingOpen`) and not a branch in `GameplayInput.escape`, because the pause menu spans gameplay AND flight AND the loop and is therefore built at the composition root where `GameplayInput` cannot see it; routing through the stack keeps ONE object owning both halves of the guarantee, so they cannot disagree about which menus exist. An unclaimed hook leaves the old answer untouched, which is what a headless scenario with no menus still gets. **The audit that preceded this found exactly ONE Escape handler in the whole client and one dead zone**: `VabPanel.ts:107` swallows every key while the design-name input has focus, which is the VAB lane's file and is reported rather than fixed. **Measured (`probes/controls.js`): 10 modals declared, 10 opened, 10 closed by Escape, `withNothingOpen: "opened the game menu"`.** The shell RESERVES Save Game, Options (Controls, Video, Audio) and Multiplayer as five visible rows, each carrying a sentence naming what it is waiting for, none of them a pressable button, because "not yet" on its own is indistinguishable from a bug. | The alternative was a per-panel Escape handler or a `pauseMenu.isOpen` test inside the existing one, and both are the shape GP-25 was written to prevent: five handlers give you four that work and one written after the rule was forgotten. (1) There is no pause anywhere in this client: `Loop.stop()` stops RENDERING too, and a real pause is a change to the tick authority, which is core-engine's and not this lane's. (2) Every other panel in the game leaves the world running, so a menu that alone froze time would be the single place "walk away and iron accumulates" stops being true, and the player would learn a rule with one exception. (3) DW-4's multiplayer makes a client-side pause impossible, so a single-player pause is a promise the game would have to break. The menu says so on its own footer rather than leaving it to be noticed. | A "pause while open" checkbox belongs in Options when Options is built, as a Loop-level feature owned by core-engine, and it should be a SETTING and not a behaviour, because the two right answers (a factory that keeps running, and a game that waits for you) are both defensible and are not the same player's answer. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |

Nothing is REFUSED: every control works in survival exactly as it does in sandbox. **It is stamped at `SaveGame.writeSlot`, the one choke point every write in the client goes through**, so a snapshot path written next month carries it without knowing it exists. Sandbox is deliberately inert, because `mode: sandbox` on the slot already says the stronger thing. **Proven across a store round trip with the in-memory record explicitly forgotten in between (`__of.cheat('forgetassist')`), which is the only way to tell a flag that came off DISK from module state that was never cleared: unmarked, then `used: ["fuel"]` at 1785211941232, then forgotten to empty, then the SAME list and the SAME timestamp back. The identical probe in sandbox records nothing while the control demonstrably fires.** | It matters at DW-27, when an achievement has to decide whether a world earned it, and on that day the question is asked of worlds that ALREADY EXIST. A flag that started being written on the day it was needed can only answer "no cheat since the update", which is not the question. GP-65 proved the same lesson three weeks earlier by putting health on every buildable before any damage source existed: retrofitting state onto live saves is the expensive half and the half you cannot do later. It is a LIST and not a boolean because "teleported once" and "flew the whole game on infinite fuel" are different claims. `restoreAssisted` UNIONS rather than replaces, so loading an older slot cannot launder the flag. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-103 | **START FRESH NAMES THE SLOT IT DESTROYS, CANNOT BE CONFIRMED BY ACCIDENT, AND VERIFIES THE WIPE AGAINST THE STORE RATHER THAN TRUSTING THE DELETE.** The confirm button does not exist in the DOM until the row is armed, and `Cheats.startFresh` refuses outright when it is not, so a caller who skips the DOM entirely is refused as well. Phase 1 built 4 buildings and 1 structural part, saved, proved the confirm cannot be skipped and fired the real button; phase 2, after a real browser reload in the same context, came back with 0 buildings and a restore ledger of `null`.** | The wipe is read back from IndexedDB rather than believed because `clearSlot` swallows its own errors by design (a save is not a rule), so a delete that silently did nothing would otherwise report success and reload into the world it claimed to destroy. The honest fix is one new export; it was not taken because a wasm rebuild would have swept two other lanes' uncommitted `of_*_api.inc` work into the binary. **Raised to Admin as a dependency.** What the rebuild costs is said out loud: the integrator's `state.timeS` restarts, and position, velocity, attitude, angular velocity, throttle, SAS mode and SAS command are all carried across explicitly. **Measured: 2190 kg full, 1974.7 kg after a burn, 2118.9 kg back, all-up mass 4405 to 4190.8 to 4335 kg, and still 2010.8 kg against a 1971 kg floor five seconds of burning later.** The assertion that makes it honest is that the PART COUNT and the STAGING COUNT are unchanged (3/1 to 3/1), because a rebuild that forgot to replay the stagings would come back with the jettisoned stages attached, refill perfectly and pass every other line. **A second defect fell out of the same measurement: `FlightSession.propellantKg()` sums cached part rows that only move on a staging, so the first version of the top-up read a number that never fell and refilled exactly once.** `livePropellantKg` re-reads `of_fl_parts`. | A cheat may be blunt; it may not be dishonest. | GP-105 | **TELEPORT TO ORBIT IS A METHOD ON THE FLIGHT SESSION'S OWN SIDE, BECAUSE THE INVARIANTS A RAW `set_pos_vel` BREAKS ARE THE SESSION'S.** `status`, `metS`, `liftedOff` and the cached telemetry rows all move with it, and every one of those was a bug found by READING `FlightSession.step` rather than by running it: `stepClamped` restores the cached position and zeroes velocity, so a teleport out of a clamp is silently undone on the next tick; `metS` stays at -1 and reads minus one second for ever; a false `liftedOff` means the arrest branch cannot fire on the way back down; and `FlightPad.stepPadClamps` leaves four arms shut around an empty pad. It sets COAST and lets /core PROMOTE it to ORBIT off its own `inSpace`/`bound`/`periapsis`, so the word on the HUD is the simulation's verdict and not the cheat's claim. **Measured: apoapsis 100000.0000000007 m, periapsis 100000.00000000012 m, eccentricity 3.69e-16, 0 m of spread, status ORBIT, observer at 96.3 km.** The speed comes from `of_gravity_accel`, the client's ONE gravity authority (DW-18/PH-18), as v = sqrt(g(r) r). **It SHUTS THE THROTTLE, and that is part of the verb: with the engine left lit the same teleport measured e = 0.15994 and 266 km between apoapsis and periapsis three seconds later.** 100 km because the atmosphere's ceiling is 60 km (`atmosphere.h topM`) and `inSpace` is that comparison. | `DebugFlight.ts` states the house rule that every debug entry goes through the player's own path, "no setter that puts a vessel in an orbit it did not fly to". So structures and the pad are the model and nothing about them changed. The two HAND MACHINES were the exception and are what Reid meant by "and stuff"; they are brought into the rule at GP-114. **THE SIX FACTORY MACHINES ARE FREE TO PLACE AND STAY FREE, and the menu SAYS `free` rather than inventing a price**: the crafted pole in the pack is a research-gate token today and is never consumed. Charging them belongs at `Factory.stage`, which is the assemblers lane's file this round, and the menu reads its prices from /core's recipe table rather than a copy, so the day that lands it shows with no change here. **RAISED TO ADMIN as the one cross-lane piece.** **THE TWO ROUTES CANNOT CHARGE DIFFERENTLY, and the reason is structural rather than careful: picking from the menu ARMS THE SAME `BuildMode` the hotbar arms, so both reach the identical commit and the identical payment. `Buildables.ts` computes no price of its own.** | The alternative was a price list in the menu, which is a second authority on cost one balance pass away from disagreeing with the thing that actually charges. Stating the boundary matters more than the code: without it, "should a wall be craftable" gets answered differently by whoever touches it next. What still gets crafted is unchanged and is now sayable in one line: tools, intermediate goods, science packs, and anything that rides a belt. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-111 | **THE BUILD MENU, ON B: EVERY BUILDABLE, PRICED, LIT IF AFFORDABLE, GREYED IF NOT, AND NEVER HIDDEN.** Reid's five clauses map one to one: B opens it (`Bindings.build`, and it joins the derived modal stack); unlocked things show; affordable is lit; click and you are holding it with the real placement ghost; Escape gets you out. **A LOCKED OR UNAFFORDABLE ROW IS STILL DRAWN, and that is the one opinion this panel holds**: seeing that a launch pad costs 60 Iron is how a player learns what to go and mine, and a menu that hid everything unaffordable would be empty on the first morning and would teach nothing. It is the same rule the tech tree already applies to a locked node. **The catalogue is DERIVED and holds no id table**: structures from `Structures.defFor`, the pad from `LaunchPads.definition`, the machines from `TYPE_ID` (a `Record<BuildKind, number>`, so a machine added next month must appear or the build stops), their prices from /core's own recipe rows via `GATED_BY_ITEM`, and the two hand machines from `GameCore.ids`. **Measured in survival: 14 buildables, 4 lit, 7 greyed, 4 locked, prices `foundation 40 Stone`, `wall 12 Wood`, `launchpad 60 Iron + 120 Stone + 20 Copper`, `hand furnace 5 Wood + 2 Raw iron`, `pole 2 Wood + 1 Copper`, `miner free`, the pad locked by `Launch Facilities` and the powered machines by `Electrification` / `Electric Smelting` BY NAME. The identical probe in sandbox: 14 lit, 0 greyed, 0 locked.** | An UNAFFORDABLE tile is still CLICKABLE, deliberately, and only a LOCKED one is not: picking up a wall you cannot pay for and reading the ghost say what it needs is more useful than a dead tile, and the placement path already refuses correctly. The catalogue holds no ids because a fourth naming authority is the mistake this project has paid for five times. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-112 | **A BUILD-MENU PICK IS AN OVERRIDE ON `Hotbar.held`, NOT A WRITE INTO A SLOT.** Reid still uses the bar and the same night's other addendum is about editing it, so a pick that silently overwrote slot 4 would destroy a loadout to place one wall. It is also not a tenth thing that decides the verb: it goes THROUGH `held`, so `partInHand`, `handInHand`, `gunInHand`, `label`, `digAllowed` and the per-tick `build.arm` all follow it with no second authority, which is GP-26's rule that the HAND decides and there is one hand. It is transient by construction: `select` and `cycle` clear it, `clearHand` clears it FIRST and on its own (because `select` cannot undo it), and `serialize` reads `bar` so it can never leak into a save. **Escape then falls out of the existing ordering with no new rule: the menu closes on the first press because it is the top modal, and the pick drops on the second because the HAND is a modal too, which is exactly Reid's "to get out of that, you press escape".** **Measured: clicking the wall tile shuts the menu, arms the ghost with `wall`, reports `fromMenu: true`, and leaves all eleven slots and the selection byte-identical; reverting the single `hold` call fails six checks by name.** | Thirteen call sites read the hotbar's hand and every one of them is correct for free, which is the whole reason the override went behind `held` rather than beside it. The one trap is `Gameplay.ts:225`, where the `hand` modal reads `partInHand !== null`: that now sees the override, which is what makes Escape drop it, and is the behaviour wanted rather than a collision. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-113 | **B MOVED OFF FREE PLACEMENT AND FREE PLACEMENT MOVED TO N.** Reid asked for the key by name. N is `stage`'s SECOND code and `stage` is a flight verb, so the collision is not one: `Gameplay.fixedStep` returns before `keys.world` while `suspended`, the build ghost cannot exist in a rocket, and on foot `stage` reaches nothing. That is exactly the precondition `Bindings.ts` already states for a code firing two actions. **`probes/build.js` pressed the literal `KeyB` and would have silently stopped toggling free placement, turning a free-placement measurement into a second measurement of snapped placement; it now presses the ACTION. `probes/wallstuck.js` already pressed the action and followed the rebind for free, which is the binding table earning its keep.** | The one file still going round the table was the one that would have broken, and it would have broken green. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-114 | **RAW MATERIALS ARE ENOUGH: A HAND FURNACE IS CRAFTED ON DEMAND AT THE MOMENT IT IS PLACED.** The two hand machines were the only buildables that still demanded a finished item in the pack, which is the crafting step Reid asked to delete. `craftOnDemand` spends /core's own recipe through /core's own `of_gp_craft` and `Machines.place` then consumes the item exactly as it always has, so there is ONE charge on ONE path. **The ORDER is the rule: a FINISHED one in the pack always wins, because it was already paid for and spending materials beside it would be charging twice for one building.** Both routes arrive at `placeMachine`, so the bar's `furnace` slot and the build menu pay the same, and the menu's tier travels on the override (`SlotContent.furnace.tier`, optional, and a saved slot never carries it so the bar keeps meaning "whatever hand machine is to hand"). **Measured in SURVIVAL with the craft panel never opened and no craft button ever pressed: harvesting alone took the furnace row from greyed to lit, a click placed it (0 to 1 machines), 54 Wood became 49, and ZERO crafted furnace items were left in the pack afterwards, which is what proves it was charged once.** | Teaching `Machines.place` to spend raw materials would have been the other way, and it would have put a second payment path beside the item one with both live at once. Making the item and consuming it in the same tick keeps exactly one place that spends and exactly one place that consumes. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-130 | **THE BUILD MENU'S BUILDINGS HAVE PICTURES, AND IT COST FIVE TABLE ROWS RATHER THAN FIVE ASSETS.** GP-111 shipped a menu that opened as a wall of words, which undercuts the whole reason for replacing craft-then-scroll with something readable at a glance. All five place an EXISTING TypeId whose art already ships, so the fix is H-7's argument word for word one family later: rows in `IconTable.ts` pointing at `foundation.glb`, `floor.glb`, `wall.glb`, `door.glb` and `launch_pad.glb`, plus FS-56's assembler picked up in passing because its own `PART_INFO` already declared `iconName: 'Assembler'` and the row was simply absent. They were pictureless because nothing had ever asked: a structural part never enters the pack (gameplay.h S.6), so the panel that draws item icons could not reach one, and `PART_INFO` carries `iconName: ''` for exactly that reason. **The menu looks them up with `forId`, not by display name**, because the id is on the `StructureDef` /core already hands over and is the identity that lasts. **Measured off the BAKER's own ledger and not off "the string is non-empty", because a canvas that rendered nothing still returns a valid PNG: Foundation 108 tris / 924 px, Floor 132 / 972, Wall 108 / 1256, Door 216 / 1098, Launch pad 2564 / 797, all of 4096, `broken: []`, and 14 of 14 tiles drawing an `<img>` rather than their own name.** The pad takes the DECK ALONE: `LaunchClamp_LOD0` is four arms generated at runtime from one shipped socket, so a row naming it would bake the single authored arm beside a 24 m deck and read as a crane. **A stale assertion fell out of the same work:** `probes/icons.js` gated its own `setupRan` on `slots.length === 9`, which stopped being true when the bar grew to ten at GP-57 and eleven at GP-86, so that probe had been reporting `valid: false` on every run since the gun shipped for a reason nobody was reading. It is now derived from the bar the client publishes. | An assertion that fails for a reason nobody reads is worth no more than one that passes for a reason nobody reads, which is why the stale gate is logged rather than quietly repaired. The pictures themselves are the cheapest possible fix and were deliberately looked for before any art was drawn: the question asked was "what already ships", not "what should we make". | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-131 | **OPTIONS / CONTROLS IS REAL, AND IT IS DERIVED FROM THE BINDING TABLE RATHER THAN DESCRIBING IT.** Every action the game listens to, with the key it is actually on, read live through `BINDINGS`, `labelOf` and `actionsFor`. `player/BindingText.ts` holds the human half (`Record<Action, {group,label}>`, exhaustive by TYPE, so an action added tomorrow must appear or the build stops) and `Bindings.ts` keeps the codes; they are split because a rebind edits one and a rewording edits the other, and neither should break the other. **Measured: 64 controls across six groups, 64 derived and 64 drawn, every row carrying at least one key, `build` reading B and `freeSnap` reading N (the rebind GP-113 made hours earlier), `use` reading `Left click` and `demolish` reading `X + Right click`.** A code shared by two actions is CALLED OUT rather than drawn twice and hoped over: 41 rows carry an "also" note, which is the walking-versus-flying overlap `Bindings.ts` permits only because one consumer is ever live, and hiding it would hide the precondition that permission rests on. **`ui/MapView.ts` was fixed in the same pass**: its hint hardcoded a literal `<b>M</b>` and now reads `labelOf('map')` / `labelOf('cancel')`, and the probe asserts THE MAP HINT AND THE CONTROLS SCREEN NAME THE SAME KEY. Rebinding is deliberately not built (Admin's steer); the shape is reserved by `ControlRow.rebindable`, false everywhere and said out loud on screen rather than implied by a control that does nothing. It is a PAGE and not a second modal, so Escape keeps meaning one thing, and the menu always reopens on its root. | This project has told the player the wrong key three times that anyone has counted: the mute hint said "(M)" for an hour after mute moved to Backslash, the map hint said a literal M for months, and the bay's launch key did nothing at all for a night. Every one is the same defect, a place that STATES a control instead of asking. A screen derived from the one source cannot be wrong about any of it, which is why showing the truth was worth building before rebinding rather than after. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-132 | **OPTIONS / VIDEO IS PUBLISHED AS A SHAPE AND IS DELIBERATELY READ ONLY.** Reid wants texture quality, render distance and similar so he can benchmark one world across machines, and every one of those knobs ALREADY EXISTS as a URL flag parsed in `Config.ts`. So the work is not "add settings", it is "give the settings that exist a screen and a way to change them", and those are two jobs with two owners: `render/**`, `Scatter*.ts`, `Config.ts` and `Boot.ts` are another lane's this round, and every knob is read ONCE at boot by code in them. `app/VideoSettings.ts` reads `Services.cfg` and NOTHING else; it touches no renderer, no material and no scatter, and it cannot change a frame. **Measured: 15 rows, all 15 drawn, every value read off the live parsed config (`quality high`, `pool 384`, `maxdepth 14`, `depth automatic (reversed-Z where available)`, `density 1x`, the post stack through `post.flags` rather than a copy), zero controls in the DOM, and the page saying "read only" in its own words.** **`applyBy` is per row and it is the handover note**: `quality`, `pool`, `maxdepth`, `depth` and `post` are boot-only by construction (a preallocated pool, a shader path) and the rest are live-settable in principle. `urlForVideo` ships now, unused by any button, exactly as `urlForMode` did before the sandbox row existed, so the wiring pass is a button rather than a design. | A screen that offered a slider for a preallocated chunk pool would be a control that cannot work, which is worse than no control. Reporting what the session is ACTUALLY running is also the thing a benchmark needs and is worth having on its own: the number you compare across two machines has to be the number each of them ran, not the default it would have used. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-133 | **`ItemIcons.ts` SPLIT INTO A TABLE AND A BAKER.** The five buildings pushed it past the 400-line cap and the seam was already there: `game/IconTable.ts` is DATA and nothing else, `ItemIcons.ts` is the renderer that turns it into pictures. The table is edited every time an item ships and the baker has not changed since it was written, so keeping them together meant every content edit re-read a WebGL context, a camera fit and a pixel-coverage check to reach a one-line row. `ICON_TABLE` and `ITEM_MESH_NODE` are RE-EXPORTED from `ItemIcons.ts`, so `BeltCargo` and every probe that imports them from there kept working unchanged and the split is invisible downstream. | Splitting on a cap is usually a smell, and this one is not, because the two halves have different edit rates and different reasons to change. Re-exporting rather than repointing the importers is the cheap half of not making it somebody else's merge conflict. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-134 | **OPTIONS / AUDIO IS THE FIRST OPTIONS PAGE THAT WRITES, AND THE FIRST SURFACE ANYWHERE THAT CAN TELL A PLAYER WHY THE GAME IS SILENT.** The difference from Video (GP-132) is the whole reason the two are built differently and it is not taste: a video knob is read ONCE at boot by files another lane owns, so that page can only report; `AudioBus` already has `setVolume`, `setMuted` and a `localStorage` key, all live, all persisted, and nothing else is editing `web/src/audio/` this round, so there is no reason for this page to be read only. **THE DIAGNOSIS IS THE PART THAT EARNS ITS KEEP.** `AudioBus`'s own header has said since it was written that every browser blocks audio until a gesture and that "the game would be mute for exactly the players who never noticed why", and until now nothing could answer that. `silentBecause` names EXACTLY ONE of the four reasons, in the order they actually gate (no Web Audio, then never unlocked, then muted, then volume zero), because they are not independent and a screen listing all four would leave the player to guess which is theirs; the page then offers the button that fixes the commonest, which works because a click on it IS the gesture the browser is waiting for. The counters sit beside it deliberately: "412 sounds asked for, none audible" and "nothing has tried to make a sound" are different faults and one number tells them apart. **Measured through the REAL controls: the slider driven as a real `input` event moved the master gain 0.7 to 0.35 on the view AND on the bus, and `localStorage['of.audio']` read `{"v":0.35,"m":false}` afterwards, which is the assertion that "a player who muted did not mean until the next refresh" is true rather than intended; mute toggled the bus and reverted; and the mute key on screen is asserted EQUAL to the key the controls screen shows for `mute`, not to a literal.** Per-bus levels are RESERVED and said out loud: `Sfx`, `Beds` and `Ambience` all connect to the one master gain, so four rows are listed with what each shares today and NONE of them carries a control, because a music slider wired to nothing is worse than no music slider. | Same trade the Video page makes, arrived at from the opposite side: publish the shape, build only what can honestly work. The ordering of `silentBecause` is the one piece of real design here, and it is worth the sentence: an unlocked context that is also muted has two true statements about it, and the useful one is the one the player can act on first. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-135 | **THE THREE OPTIONS PAGES MOVED OUT OF `Cheats.ts` INTO `app/OptionsPages.ts`.** They lived on `Cheats.view` for exactly as long as there was one of them; the third pushed that file past its 400-line cap, which is the usual signal that a responsibility had been sharing a house with a different one. `Cheats` owns the TESTING controls and their consequences (GP-102's assisted mark, GP-103's armed confirm); `OptionsPages` owns the pages that only read and write settings a player is entitled to change. The audio verbs still route through `Cheats.press`, and that is deliberate rather than left over: one place a button press is handled is one place it can be wrong about what it did, which is the defect GP-103 caught in this exact function on its first run. **The null-world fallback is a REAL `AudioBus` rather than a hand-written empty view**, because the alternative is a second implementation of the view's shape that would drift; a fresh bus reports `supported`, `unlocked: false` and the stored volume, which is exactly the truth about a world with no sound in it. | Splitting on a cap is usually a smell and this one is not, for the same reason GP-133's was not: the two halves have different edit rates and different reasons to change. | GP-136 | **A SAVE SLOT IS KEYED BY MODE AND NAME: `save:<mode>:<name>`.** Admin's call and the right one, and it is DW-31's own argument one layer out: sandbox and survival are different rulesets, so a flat list invites loading a sandbox world into a survival session, which is either a crash or a silent cheat. Keying by mode makes that UNREPRESENTABLE rather than validated, and the load list then shows only the running mode's saves, because a greyed row a player cannot use is just a question they have to answer. The `save:` prefix keeps the two families apart in one flat store, so `auto` and `auto-sandbox` can never be listed, loaded or deleted as if they were somebody's named save. **THE AUTOSAVE KEY IS NOT MIGRATED**: it is the slot the game writes unprompted, it already has the right key, and `SAVE_VERSION` does not move for any of this, so every world anybody is playing keeps working untouched. **A COLON IS REFUSED IN A NAME RATHER THAN ESCAPED**, because escaping means the writer and the parser have to agree forever and this key is read BACK to build the list, so a disagreement would surface as a save the player can see and cannot open; the refusal is a whole sentence and nothing is written for it (measured: `a name cannot contain a colon`, `saved` unchanged, `refusals` up). **The list is DERIVED from the store's own keys** (`allKeys()` then filter), never from a list the client keeps, so a slot written or deleted by any path at all appears or disappears without anything registering it: the same rule `ModalStack` applies to menus and `HealthCensus` to buildings. `SaveGame.ts` gained exactly two exported seams (`slotKey`, `tx`) plus `stampAssisted`, so the named layer shares the one store connection and the one authority on GP-102's mark; the factory lane's `chestStore` in that file was untouched. | The alternative, one list with a mode field and a refusal on mismatch, is the design DW-31 already rejected once for the autosave and for the same reason: a check that can be forgotten is worse than a shape that cannot be built. Putting the mode in the key costs nothing and removes the question. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-137 | **NAMED SAVES, MANUAL SAVE, A LOAD LIST AND DELETE, AND LOADING IS A COPY PLUS A RELOAD.** GP-103 argued the reload for Start Fresh and the argument is stronger here: `Persist.apply` restores a diff over a FRESHLY GENERATED world, which is the only state a boot can be in, so applying a save over a live world would mean emptying the factory, the base, the tunnels, the pads, the health book, the pack and the progression spine first, in an order that matters, and the failure mode of getting one wrong is a HALF LOADED world that cannot even be reported. Copying the chosen slot onto the autosave key and reloading reuses the boot path exactly. **PROVEN IN TWO PHASES WITH A NEGATIVE CONTROL, which is the whole proof: two runs identical up to ONE BUTTON PRESS. After a real browser reload: 5 buildings back, against 0.** Without the control, "the world came back" is satisfied by a load that did nothing, because the autosave would have restored something either way. **DELETE SHIPS WITH THE LIST**, because a list with no delete fills up and the player cannot fix it; it is ARMED then FIRED because Load and Delete sit two centimetres apart on the same row, and the unarmed verb is refused while its button does not exist in the DOM at all. The AUTOSAVE ROW HAS NO DELETE: Start Fresh is that verb and it says what it destroys, so a second smaller one here would be the more dangerous of the two. **Three real defects were found by driving it rather than reading it.** The typed name round-tripped through `press`, which calls `menu.invalidate()`, which rebuilt the page and WIPED THE BOX THE PLAYER WAS TYPING IN; a text input already holds its own value and now hands it over on the press, so the refusal arrives one press later in a whole sentence. The render key omitted the saves view, so the page would have drawn once and never again and a save would land in the store and never appear on the screen that exists to show it. And the list was built once per session, so the autosave never appeared at all: it now re-reads the store on the rising edge of the page being shown, which is the difference between a list and a photograph of one. | Every one of the three was invisible to reading and obvious to a driven click, which is the argument for probes that press the thing a player presses rather than calling the function behind it. The third was found in a SCREENSHOT, by an autosave row that was missing with no way to tell that from there being none. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-138 | **THE OPTION PAGES' HTML SPLIT OUT OF `PauseMenu.ts` INTO `ui/OptionPagesHtml.ts` WHEN SAVE GAME MADE IT FOUR.** `PauseMenu` owns the SHELL: the frame, the modal registration, the DOM half of the pointer transition, the root page and its testing block. The four sub-pages have nothing in common with the shell and everything in common with each other, each taking plain data and returning a string with no state and no opinion. It is the same seam `app/OptionsPages.ts` draws one layer up where the DATA is derived, and it is drawn in the same place deliberately. **`Cheats.restartOff` was deleted in the same pass and the suppression moved to the PORT**, which was a real bug rather than tidying: Start Fresh and Load both restart, and a flag only Start Fresh consulted let a driven Load navigate out from under the probe that was reporting on it, which is exactly how it was found. | GP-139 | **THE PAD TELLS YOU WHAT TO DO, STANDING, AND IT NEVER NAMES A CONTROL THAT IS ALREADY AT ITS STOP.** Reid built a rocket, rolled it out, sat looking at a chip reading `CLAMPED` and stopped. The sentences already existed, in `FlightClamp.clampHoldReason`, and he never saw one: they are produced only when a press is REFUSED, so the explanation was reachable exactly by the players who did not need it. `sim/LaunchSteps.ts` derives ONE instruction from state every frame and draws it until it stops being true, which is `AudioSettings.silentBecause`'s shape and argument (a condition that only speaks when poked is a condition nobody hears). **THE ORDERING IS THE DESIGN AND THE ACCEPTANCE ASSERTS THE NEGATIVE DIRECTLY.** `clampHoldReason`'s own header records what a wrong order costs: one line that said "throttle up" in all four clamp states, which Reid read at 100% throttle with no engine lit, naming the control already at its stop while the control that would have freed him was being refused by a guard. So the probe walks the pad states and asserts at every one that the guide names no control at its limit, and it asserts that on the ACTION NAME (`stepNames`, published for the purpose) rather than on the prose, because prose is meant to be reworded and an assertion that breaks on a better sentence is one nobody keeps. **Measured, and the third row is the one that matters: `CLAMPED` at 0% throttle gives `Press Space to light the first stage`; `CLAMPED` at 100% THROTTLE with nothing lit gives THE SAME SENTENCE and never the word throttle; engine lit at 0% gives `Engine lit. Hold Left Shift to throttle up`; under power with fuel gives NOTHING, because nothing is owed; and `ORBIT` gives `In orbit. M opens the map`.** Four distinct instructions across one run is what tells a live derivation from a constant. The chip is read off the ELEMENT so the assertion is the painted pixel against the derivation, and it is drawn FIRST of the three chips, ahead of the standing warning and the transient flash, because a narrow window truncates from the right and whatever else is on that row the thing to press has to survive. | It is a THIRD kind of thing and not a synonym for the two chips beside it: `warning` says something is wrong, `message` is a transient flash, and this says which key to press; all three can be true at once, and a player told the vessel cannot be saved still needs to know how to light the engine. '' is a real answer rather than a gap, because a line that always says something is a line players stop reading. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |
| GP-140 | **ONE CODE, ONE SPELLING: `labelOf` and the controls screen shared two prettifiers and disagreed.** `labelOf` turned `ShiftLeft` into "Shift" for the one-key hints and `BindingText.prettyCode` turned it into "Left Shift" for the controls screen, so the launch guide said `Hold Shift to throttle up` while the screen that lists every control said `Left Shift`. It is the defect class `Bindings.ts` already carries three scars from (the mute hint, the map hint, H-5's raw key codes) one level further in: not a second copy of WHICH key, but a second copy of how to SAY it. Driven on the shipped build: hold an engine, put the cursor on the drawn pixel of a tank's bottom face, and `snapped` reads `bottom` while the frame is **bit-identical** to the frame with the cursor parked on empty space. **Zero pixels, twice, on two stacks and at two node heights, against 625 for the same part going on top and 1259 for it going on the side.** The cause is one line of framing: the pad is an opaque 4.2 m disc and `setFloor` put its top face exactly on the lowest COMMITTED part, so the preview of a downward attachment, which by construction hangs below that, was drawn entirely underneath it. Discriminated rather than assumed: from a camera below the floor plane the same ghost moved 320 pixels, so it was always drawn and always hidden. `VabView.applyFloor` now puts the floor under the lowest of three things, the committed assembly, the drawn node markers and the ghost. The marker clearance is the half that happens BEFORE the player hovers anything: a stack node sits on the axis of the face it belongs to, so the lowest one is buried between the pad below it and the part's own hull above, and the one marker saying "you may attach here" is the one a player building downward never sees. After: bottom face 0 to 261 green pixels, a third part under a stack already hanging below the origin 0 to 260, top unchanged at 624, radial unchanged at 1243, and bit-exactly no green with an empty hand. | `showNodes` already carried the argument in its own comment, "an invisible snap is indistinguishable from a broken one"; this is that rule applied to the thing standing in front of it. | GP-142 | **EVERY STRAP-ON SLID 2.8571 m DOWN THE ROCKET ON SAVE AND LOAD, BECAUSE `off` WAS THE LITERAL 0.** `VesselDesign.toJson` wrote `off: 0` for every part, so `radialOffsetM`, the one number that says where along the hull a strap-on sits, was never serialised. | GP-147 | **THE HANG-UNDER VARIANT IS PROVABLY REDUNDANT, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS WHY IT WAS NOT BUILT.** Admin ruled for "place a part and let the existing stack hang under it", on my own recommendation. Driven before building it: the SAME physical rocket built top-down and bottom-up produced **identical** unplaceable sets (the same 6 parts by name), identical stack-reachable lists, identical free-node counts and identical refusals. Rootness is invisible to `snapAtRay` and to `fitAt`. Hang-under attaches the OLD ROOT's top socket to the new part's bottom socket, which is the same socket pair as attaching the new part to the old root's free `top` NODE, so the two operations succeed and fail on exactly the same condition: measured, the root pod with a free top accepted 11 parts by both routes, and the root engine with its top occupied accepted none by either. **A second path to an existing capability is a second authority**, which is the failure this project has paid for repeatedly. And in the one case that IS genuinely blocked, an engine going upward (GP-145), hang-under does not help either, because the block is a missing socket on the part rather than a missing operation. | Recorded because Admin adopted my own recommendation and the measurement then refuted it, which is worth more in the log than a quiet substitution. | GP-148 | **THE ROOT IS NOW THE TOP OF THE STACK, NOT WHATEVER THE PLAYER PLACED FIRST.** Admin ruled for GP-146 rather than deferring it, and this changes what the root means, deliberately. Before: the root was whichever part went down first, invisible to `snapAtRay` and `fitAt` (measured, the same rocket built from either end gives identical placeable sets and identical refusals) and observable in exactly one place, destructively. `of_vs_remove` deletes the subtree FURTHER FROM THE ROOT, so an identical three-part rocket lost its **Main Engine** built one way and its **Command Pod** built the other, from the same right click on the same part. After: `VesselReroot.normaliseRoot` puts the root on the topmost part of the stack spine on every attach and every remove, so a delete always takes what is below. **Not the general re-root Admin refused**: that refusal was about the WRITE path, since an in-place `parent` rewrite leaves the old root early in the array and `fromJson` resolves parents by index, so every save would drop the old root and its descendants. This rebuilds through `toJson`/`fromJson` in BFS order from the new root, so parents precede children by construction, and it PERMUTES the existing json rather than composing a second one, so the radial offset, the per-part stage and the latch travel for free. Scoped by an OPTIONAL constructor argument: the bay opts in, `FlightVessels.ts` deliberately does not, because a vessel that has flown must not have its tree rearranged underneath it. | Not a new rule but one already written down and silently untrue: `vessel.h` section 6 says the root is the command pod so that further from the root is further down the stack, and GP-145 measured that the assembly order which WORKS for a multi-stage rocket is top-down, so the orders that produced a wrong root were the ordinary ones. | GP-152 | **ANY KEYPRESS DESTROYED THE FOCUSED ROW, which made keyboard navigation impossible before it was designed.** `PauseMenu.render` replaces `body.innerHTML` whenever its diff key moves, so the focused button was removed from the document and focus fell back to BODY. **Discriminated rather than assumed**, because "the key blurred it" and "the rebuild ate it" want different fixes: focus SURVIVES ten ticks with no key pressed, SURVIVES the keydown synchronously, and is gone two ticks later with `document.contains(button) === false`. Tab to a row, press anything at all, and the row you were on no longer exists. `render` now remembers the focused row's `data-cheat` before the rebuild and restores it after: **the id and not a node or an index**, because the list gains and loses rows (a blocked cheat, an armed confirm) and the row a player was on keeps its identity through that. When the row really has gone it falls back to the first. | "The row you were on has gone" and "the keyboard does nothing" look identical to a player and only one of them is recoverable without a mouse. | GP-155 | **`startfresh` WAS TWO DEFECTS, AND BOTH WERE THE SAME REBUILD EATING SOMETHING.** (1) Two receipts carry the id `startfresh:confirm`, the synchronous acknowledgement and `startFresh`'s own terminal one after two IndexedDB round trips, and they were told apart ONLY by whether `detail` happened to be present. The probe slept a fixed 0.6 and popped, which is a race against a store: the terminal receipt lands about 190 ms after the press on a quiet machine and later on a busy one, so it popped the acknowledgement and read `detail.slotRemains` off `undefined`. Fixed at both ends: the acknowledgement now carries `{ pending: true }` so **"not yet" and "no" are no longer the same absence, on the one verb in the game that destroys a save**, and the probe polls for the terminal receipt and fails BY NAME if it never arrives. GP-116 wrote "a 1.25 m booster stands 0.15 m clear of a 1.25 m core", which was true of the mount PLANE and false of the MESH; it is now true of both, and the number it always claimed is the number that comes out. **The positive control is untouched** and is what separates "the convention changed" from "the defect spread": the fin and the solar panel still sit with their origin exactly on the hull, 0.625 against 0.625, unchanged to the last digit. | Accepted | 2026-07-28 |

| GP-73 | **THE PAD'S ONE-PRESS RULE IS "IS THERE ANYTHING TO LOSE?", NOT "HAVE YOU PRESSED BEFORE?", and PH-29's assumption is refuted by measurement rather than argued with.** `sim/FlightClamp.ts` replaces PH-29's `status === 'CLAMPED' && stagings > 0` with `litStageThrustN(s) <= 0`: a second stage press while the clamp holds is refused only when the burn that is ALREADY RUNNING actually makes thrust, because only then is there an engine to throw away. PH-29's reason was sound and its ASSUMPTION was that the first stage always contains the engine. It need not, and the counter-example is a joint **GP-32 explicitly permits**: a stack decoupler hung under an engine bell. `of_vs_autostage` groups decouplers by tree depth and gives burn k the engines in the deepest remaining group's SUBTREE, so a decoupler with nothing below it owns a burn 0 containing exactly itself. Driven in the shipped bay, sandbox, **`handStaged: false`**, which is what says autostage did this and not a stale table: pod / tank / engine / decoupler gives **burn 0: 0 engines, 1 part, 0 N, 0 m/s, 0 s** against **burn 1: 1 engine, 200000 N, TWR 3.7026, 2166.92 m/s**. That is the shape of Reid's HUD exactly (stage 0 dv 0 TWR 0.00 burn 0s, stage 1 dv 378 TWR 4.91 burn 6s, 53.21 t). **THE THRUST IS READ OFF THE AS-BUILT STAGE TABLE AND NOT OFF `telemetry.thrustN`, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE FIX.** Telemetry is throttle-scaled and PH-29's measured case is four presses with the throttle SHUT, so a live-thrust predicate reads zero there, waves all four through and re-breaks the exact case the guard exists for. The two requirements pull opposite ways and this is the only reading that satisfies both. **The hold message is now four sentences instead of one**, because the four states ask for four different things: nothing lit yet, the lit burn has no engine ("stage 0 has no engine, stage again"), the throttle is down, or it genuinely cannot lift at this throttle (which names the recovery key, GP-74). The old single line said "TWR 0.00, throttle up" in all four and Reid read it at 100% throttle, which is the state it is most wrong about: it names the one control already at its stop while the control that would free him was being refused three lines above. `probes/stagelock.js` asserts BOTH halves in one run, and the negative control is the proof: reverting ONLY that one predicate to `stagings > 0` fails three assertions by name (`B: PRESS 2 GOES THROUGH: 1`, `B: THE CLAMP RELEASES: CLAMPED / 1 -> 1`, `B: AND IT LEAVES THE GROUND: 0 m`) and reproduces the deadlock verbatim, while section A stays green. | A guard whose predicate is "has the player done this before" is a guard about the PLAYER; the thing it protects is a property of the VEHICLE, and the two coincide only on vehicles whose first stage holds an engine. Stating the rule over what the vehicle can lose makes it true on every vehicle, including ones nobody has built yet. Autostage is deliberately left alone: a burn that only fires decouplers is a legitimate stage (a fairing, a spent tank with no engine), so collapsing empties in the bay would be a mis-fix that also broke the honest cases, and this lane may not touch `web/wasm/` in any case. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-74 | **THERE IS A WAY OUT, AND IT IS ONE VERB: `recover`.** `app/FlightRecover.ts`, on the `recover` action (`Delete`), routed in `Systems.ts` beside the key that made the vessel, reachable on foot, from the cockpit and from the map. It destroys the live vessel, gives the pad back (`reclamp`, so a cleared pad is in the same state as one never used, which is the only state a save is allowed to describe) and LEAVES THE DESIGN in the bay. Before this the only exits from flight were `board` and `disembark` and neither removed anything, so PH-29's own escape route was to walk 200 m and press the board key to overwrite the rocket: build, fly, adjust, which is most of what an assembly bay is for, went through that walk every time. **ONE VERB AND NOT THREE.** "Clear the pad", "revert to the bay" and "recover the vessel" are the same operation seen from three places; the differences are entirely where the player is standing and which panel they open next. **IT DOES NOT REFUND, AND THAT IS THE DECISION RATHER THAN AN OMISSION.** A refund would be right if a roll-out cost anything: it does not. Parts are paid for once, in the bay, at `of_vs_part_pay`, and `rollOut` copies the paid-for design and charges nothing (GP-34 names the neighbouring hole). Crediting the parts back would MINT them: build once, roll out, recover, repeat. The design is still yours and rolling it out again is still free, which is the sense in which nothing was lost, and the message says so. Sandbox does not differ, because `freeBuild` means there was nothing to give back in either mode. **It goes THROUGH `disembark` rather than around it**, so "cannot get out in flight" still means something and there is no second, weaker copy of that rule. Measured (`probes/stagelock.js`): recovering with no vessel is refused; recovering a vessel at **58 m and 32.63 m/s** is refused with the sentence that already existed and `recoveries` stays **0**; recovering the same craft once it is `DOWN` after a **4790.4 m** flight returns 11 parts, `live false`, `aboard false`, `recoveries 1`, the pack string **identical** before and after, and the 11-part design still in the bay. | The pad already knew what stood on it and the model already had `socket_vessel`; what was missing was a verb, and the absence of one is what turned GP-73's deadlock from an annoyance into a dead end. `Delete` had to be a non-letter because every one of the 26 letter keys is already bound in `Bindings.ts`, on foot or in a rocket or both; `Backspace` is deliberately NOT a second code, because the bay carries a text input and a key that sometimes eats your typing is worse than a key some keyboards make awkward. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-75 | **THE HAND-STAGING LATCH TRAVELS WITH THE DESIGN (`DesignJson.hs`) INSTEAD OF BEING SET ON EVERY BOOT.** `Vab.create` used to write `handStaged = true` unconditionally whenever it restored a work in progress, and `loadNamed` did the same for any named design. That is not GP-33's rule ("the moment a stage arrow is pressed") but a second, permanent one: after ANY page reload autostage never ran again, so a part placed afterwards kept whatever stage group `of_vs_attach` gave it and the table quietly stopped describing the rocket. The field is optional and absent reads false, so an older slot is legal and simply resumes deriving. **THIS IS NOT WHAT BIT REID, AND THE PROBE SAYS SO OUT LOUD**: GP-73's section B asserts `handStaged === false` on the vehicle that produced the empty burn, so the diagnosis is autostage's rule and not a stale latch. It is a second road to the same place and it was one field to close while the file was open. Measured (`probes/stagelock.js` section D): the latch reads **false -> true -> false -> true** across build, stage arrow, load-the-derived-design, load-the-hand-ordered-design. | A latch that protects a table is state ABOUT that table and belongs in the same object; keeping it in the session meant the two could disagree the moment a page reloaded and nothing would say so. Also worth stating: a blueprint whose author never touched the arrows should keep re-deriving in the hands of whoever loads it, which the old blanket `true` denied. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-76 | **THE LAUNCH PAD'S STAIRS ARE WALKABLE, AND NOTHING IN THIS PROJECT USED TO CHECK THAT A CONTRACT'S DECLARED `col_*` SET IS WHAT THE `.glb` ACTUALLY SHIPS.** Reid: "the stairs on the launch pad dont work". `tools/blender/build_launch_pad.py` deliberately stopped BOTH deck-bank proxies short of the stair notch (`STAIR_S = 9.20`), so the stair run was **2.72 m of pad with nothing under it**, ending in the 2.00 m south face of `col_LaunchTrench`: the player gained the foundation deck's 0.50 m, crossed air at pad-base height and wedged against a wall, **7 of 25 walk slices refused, never reaching the deck in 5 s**. Now eight `col_LaunchStep1..8` boxes generated by the SAME `stair_treads()` the drawn geometry reads, so the two cannot drift, with a **0.25 m** riser against `STRUCTURE_STEP_UP_M[0] = 0.55` so the walker needs no change (a ramp is not expressible: `StructureBodies.inside` is a plain AABB min/max test). Measured: height gained above the pad's own base plane **0.000 m before, 2.000 m after** (which is `socket_vessel`, read off the asset), 2.500 m total from the terrain, refusals **7 -> 0**, the deck reached in 1.2 s, 20 -> 24 structure tests per tick, and the profile rises 0.25 / 0.45 / 0.75 / 1.00 / 1.45 / 1.75 / 2.00. **TWO OF THE BRIEFED PREMISES WERE WRONG AND THE MEASUREMENT CORRECTED BOTH**: the GLB ships FIVE proxies and not three, so the flame trench is not a fall-through (`col_LaunchTrench` is the misleadingly named east deck bank and `col_LaunchMount` is the launch table over the trench); and the contract mismatch runs the OTHER WAY, the file shipped `col_LaunchMount` that the contract never declared. **The GLB is authoritative and the contract was corrected**, because the proxy is real, correctly placed and load-bearing, and churning a shipped node name for cosmetics is a worse trade. `web/scripts/check-proxies.mjs` is the general fix, FIRST in `npm run check` so it actually runs, with three rules: declared-but-absent, shipped-but-undeclared, and a refusal of any `col_*` name ending `_<digits>` because `proxiesOf` strips that suffix and would silently collapse eight tread boxes into one. Across all 48 assets and 85 proxies it found **exactly one** problem and it was the pad. | The stairs are the one part of a 24 m building a player meets on foot, and they were the only part with no collision at all, which is the signature of geometry authored for the eye and never for the body. The general check is worth more than the fix: a contract that DECLARES a proxy set and is never compared against the file documents intentions rather than facts. | Accepted | 2026-07-27 |
| GP-261 | **THE AUTOPILOT'S TARGET LIST HAS ONE ROW TYPE AND THREE SOURCES, so a planetary body is never a special case bolted on.** `game/AutopilotTargets.ts` publishes `AutopilotTarget` (id, kind, name, detail, `orbit` OR `blocked`, never both, never neither) and `TargetSource`. The vessel registry is a source, the body table is a source, and the player's own requested orbit is a source. **Reid's fifth ask (set an automatic take it to this orbit) is therefore not a second feature: it is a ROW**, which means the reach gate, the departure chart and the scheduler are ONE code path for both asks, and a third kind is a third source. | GP-271 | **THE SCHEDULING GATE IS PER DEPARTURE TIME AND NEVER GLOBAL**, which is Reid's rule verbatim and the reason the chart exists. Three states: `go`, `wait` (you cannot fly THIS departure but there is one you can, so it is a schedule and not a refusal) and `never` (no departure is affordable, which is about the vehicle and not the clock). `firstFeasibleIndex` and `bestIndex` stay separate because the earliest flyable window and the cheapest one are different questions, and a screen that conflated them would offer to schedule a departure the player still cannot afford. Measured on a real rendezvous: 52 of 64 departures flyable, 12 not, the arm button's `disabled` EQUAL to `!chosenFeasible` at all 64. **Distance cannot produce a refusal in flight** (a two-burn Hohmann from 680 km reaches every bound orbit around Forge for about 1222 m/s), so a bad departure is what exercises the refusing branch, not a far target. Accepted 2026-08-03. |
| GP-272 | **THE EXECUTION CONTRACT WENT THE OTHER WAY, AND THAT IS THE INTERESTING PART.** The PLANNING contract was published by this lane first, in `game/Autopilot.ts`'s header, committed before the other side existed; physics answered it exactly. **Execution went physics-first** (`web/wasm/of_ap_api.inc` section 21.5, PH-156 to PH-158, commit `1e95eec`), and that comment is the specification. `game/AutopilotRun.ts` BINDS it: five exports (`of_ap_arm_hold_orbit`, `of_ap_arm_transfer`, `of_ap_cancel`, `of_ap_status`, `of_ap_note`), the 18-word status row typed field for field with no renaming on the way across, symbol-presence detection reporting the C names, and a PENDING state. **Nothing is restated and nothing is paraphrased**; where this lane wanted something the row does not carry it is a NAMED ASK at the bottom of the file (GP-276) rather than an invention here. **Word 0 is `running`, not a constant 1**: the WORD COUNT says whether anything is armed, so a refused arm still returns 18 words with `running` 0 and phase Aborted and the screen can show the refusal instead of forgetting the press. Reading word 0 as "armed" would have made every finished and every refused programme indistinguishable from never having pressed the button. ABI stays 22 throughout; additive, no stride moves. **THE PENDING STATE IS PROVEN WITH NO CONTRIVANCE AT ALL**, because HEAD's COMMITTED `of-core.wasm` carries zero `of_ap_*` symbols, planning or execution: served that exact binary, the same client takes the pending branch and the screen prints **`of_ap_arm_hold_orbit, of_ap_arm_transfer, of_ap_cancel, of_ap_status, of_ap_note`** by name, with the arm button dead and nothing armed. **Both seams speak at once in that build**, deliberately: GP-139's "one thing at a time" is a rule about instructing a PLAYER, and the audience for a missing-export sentence is whoever has to add the export, so telling them about the planner and making them fix it to discover the executor is also missing wastes an hour. Accepted 2026-08-03. |

| GP-274 | **CANCEL PUBLISHES THE RESIDUAL, AND IT IS SAMPLED BEFORE THE CALL.** Physics cuts the throttle inside `of_ap_cancel` and says so; that half would otherwise have been a runaway engine, because `Autopilot::disarm` touches only the executor and a caller that merely stopped driving it would leave `sim.state.throttle` where the last Command put it, for ever. **This lane's half is the RESIDUAL.** A cancel mid-burn leaves an orbit that is neither the one you had nor the one you planned, and one frame later `of_ap_status` returns 0 words and `of_ap_note` says "nothing armed", so **"I stopped you 122.6 m/s into a burn" and "nothing ever happened here" are the same state**. | GP-275 | **TIME WARP IS HELD AT 1x FOR THE WHOLE POWERED PHASE, AND IT IS A VISIBILITY RULE RATHER THAN A CORRECTNESS ONE.** Physics guarantees the programme flies identically under warp (the executor runs inside every sub-step of `of_fl_step_n`, measured at 200 ticks a call against single stepping). At 1000x a five-second burn happens inside a fifth of ONE FRAME: the player never sees the engine light, never sees the countdown, cannot cancel, and then finds a different orbit with no event on screen that produced it. **The condition is the executor's own PHASE and never a time threshold**, because "drop warp 60 seconds out" would be `kOrientLeadS` copied into this client, which is the constant-copying INSTRUMENTS.md keeps catching; asking which phase it is in gets the same answer for free and stays right if physics retunes the lead. **The first version was wrong and only driving it showed how**: it dropped warp ONCE, guarded on an edge, and warp is a LADDER the player steps up, so the very next press undid it and the burn ran at 200x with the rule believing it had already fired. A rule that must HOLD a condition has to be evaluated while the condition holds. Accepted 2026-08-03. |

The quote is latched at the instant the button goes down and drawn beside the programme, with **no threshold and no constant**, so the player sees them agree instead of this client owning a copy of the reserve fraction to reconcile them with. | GP-282 | **THE PICTURE FOUND ONE THE NUMBERS COULD NOT (DW-7).** `probes/apshot.js` composes rather than asserts: it arms a hold-orbit and stops the run a quarter of the way through the first burn, so the frame `run.mjs` captures is the feature rather than whatever state a long assertion run happened to end in. The number was correct: the current burn's ignition time is in the past for the whole time it is burning, so the negative branch fired and the row was true and meaningless. **Overdue means "the engine should be lit and is not", which is a Coast or Orient statement**, and once it IS lit the bar and the spend carry everything. No metric would have caught this and no assertion in `apexec.js` was even wrong; the row is now drawn only before the burn. **And reading the same frame's FIGURES back against the executor found a second one**: `this burn 34.2 of 136.3 m/s` was drawing the PROGRAMME's total spend against the CURRENT burn's size. | GP-284 | **A SOCKET IS A FRAME, NOT A POINT, AND THIS FILE WAS THROWING THE ROTATION ON THE FLOOR.** `learnStationSockets` did `setFromMatrixPosition` into a `Map<string, [number, number, number]>`, which was harmless while the only consumer was `stationStandLocal` (it wants somewhere to put a pair of feet and does not care which way anything points) and stopped being harmless the moment docking became real: **two hulls meeting nose to nose and two hulls meeting nose to tail have identical socket POSITIONS**, so physics cannot aim a capture at one. The frame is now `{pos, face, roll}` in station-local metres, and **the convention is not restated here, it is read from the same matrix columns the asset lane's own gate reads**: `validate_glb`'s `socket_frames` block takes `roll` from columns 0..2 and `face` from 8..10, the empty's local +X and +Z. Both fall out of ONE composite (`inv * o.matrixWorld`) rather than three extractions that could disagree about handedness, which also sidesteps a trap the old habit would have walked into: **`applyMatrix4` on a `Vector3` is affine and carries the translation**, so a direction run through it comes back offset by the station's own position and then normalised, which is a plausible-looking axis that is not the one in the asset. `stationSocket` still returns the position unchanged, so every existing caller is untouched. **Why it was invisible for so long is the part worth keeping:** RN-853 found the station's dock authored 180 degrees out, facing back into its own hull, and nothing caught it because the asset gate compared positions and had no opinion about axes, and nothing in the client could have caught it either because the client discarded the axis before anything could look. **Two independent checks that are both blind in the same way are not two checks.** Accepted 2026-08-03. |
| GP-285 | **THE AXIS REACHES RUNTIME, AND THAT IS A DIFFERENT CLAIM FROM THE BYTES BEING RIGHT.** RN-854's offline gate proves the shipped `.glb` is correct; it cannot prove the axis survives the loader, the scene graph and this client's own station-local transform, and until GP-284 it did not. `probes/stationdock.js` reads the RUNNING client's published frames and compares them against `contracts.json`, **the same declaration `validate_glb` measures the bytes against, so the two halves are checked against ONE contract rather than against each other**. Measured: `socket_dock` at (30.399999618530273, 2.200000047683716, 0) with **face dot 1, roll dot 1, and face-roll perpendicularity 0**, against a contract of pos (30.40, 2.20, 0) face +X roll +Y; `socket_entry` face dot 0.9999999999999911. Predicted first from the shipped bytes offline and then reproduced at runtime, rather than measured and then explained. **Asserted as DOT PRODUCTS** because that is the quantity ASSET-SPECS 4.23's anti-parallel rule is stated in and because a component comparison passes on a vector that is right about every axis and wrong about its length; and the face-roll perpendicularity is asserted separately, because two nearly parallel axes describe no orientation at all and neither dot-against-contract can see that. **The antecedent is asserted before any axis is looked at** (the socket count and the presence of `socket_dock`), since every loop below it would otherwise be `[].every(...)`. **The negative control is the state that shipped**: discarding the rotation exactly as `setFromMatrixPosition` did goes red on **exactly the three axis assertions**, printing `dot = 0.000000` and naming the RN-853 defect, while every POSITION check stays green, which is the correct signature for a bug that got the position right; revert byte-identical (`a93304c6797954716f77b6d0a0aad0013684b762`). Accepted 2026-08-03. |

An `Objective` gains `moot(g)`, returning the SENTENCE saying why this world cannot satisfy it, and a moot card is **drawn and explained rather than filtered out** (GP-114: a locked thing named beats an absent thing) and **stepped past rather than waited on**. **ONE AUTHORITY, TWO READERS:** the airless test existed in `StarterContent`'s invariant and a SECOND time inline in `Gameplay.reset`, which is two expressions of one fact, correct together until somebody changes one; `bodyIsAirless` is now the only copy and both call it, so a body that grows an atmosphere gets its trees and its tutorial back in the same breath. **Driven on BOTH bodies, which is the only shape that means anything**: a build marking every card moot passes a Cinder-only run and a build marking none passes a Forge-only one. Forge reads `mootCount 0`, the wood card live and current with the hint `aim at one and hold Left click`; Cinder reads `mootCount 1`, `woodPlaceable false`, the reason drawn where the hint would be, and the player pointed at `Craft a pickaxe`. **The probe caught a defect in the fix**: `view()` published `done: true` for the skipped row, because `index` is a POSITION and `i < index` had been standing in for a claim of achievement. Measured with a foundation in hand across a pitch sweep: **0 deg 6.051 m outside, -30 deg 3.206 m outside, -60 deg 1.645 m INSIDE, -85 deg 1.385 m INSIDE**. From the player's chair that is exactly Reid's two sentences: "snapping is broken" (you aim at the seam and nothing happens, because there is nothing there) and "you can only build bottom-up" (every revision means demolishing back to it). **GP-145 measured the DIRECTION constraint and was right about it, and the direction was never the whole complaint**, because the reference vehicle is always built once, from nothing, in a single pass, and **a fixture that never revises cannot exhibit this**. That is why two lanes fixed something real and the report survived both. `suppressedNodes` publishes what was withheld, so the bay can say "there IS a face here and it is taken" instead of saying nothing, and so a probe can point at a seam and assert a count rather than infer it from a silence. **INSTRUMENT ONLY, NOT THE FIX**: insertion is a feature (re-parent the existing child onto the arriving part) and it is not a thing to design at the end of a pass. | GP-293 | **INSERTING INTO A STACK NEEDS NOTHING FROM PHYSICS, WHICH IS WHY IT IS DONE.** A design is a FLAT ARRAY whose `parent` is an INDEX into itself and `VesselDesign.fromJson` rebuilds one by walking it in order; GP-148 already leaned on exactly that for the re-root. So insertion is not surgery on a live tree, it is a SPLICE plus the rebuild that already exists: **no `of_vs_reparent`, no ABI bump, and no waiting on a lane live in two other headers tonight.** `VesselInsert.insertAt` is three edits and an index remap: the arriving part takes the child's parent and attach, the child's parent becomes the arriving part and it KEEPS its attach, and the new row is spliced in BEFORE the child because `fromJson` resolves a parent out of handles it has already built. **The remap is the part that bites**: the stage lists are indices into the same array, they are easy to forget, and forgetting them does not throw. It moves a part into the wrong stage and surfaces on the pad as a rocket that drops the wrong half. **Three refusals, each a sentence and each reachable**: the root (nothing is above it, and GP-148 made the root the top of the stack so that face is genuinely free), a radial mount (a strap-on carries an angle and a height and splicing a stack part into that seam would have to invent what those mean), and a design stored with a part before its own parent (refused rather than reordered here, because this file does not own that order). | GP-294 | **A FIXTURE THAT REVISES, AND IT IS THE DELIVERABLE.** Two lanes measured the bay, both found something real, both fixed it, and Reid's complaint survived both.
